                A positive resolution of De Giorgi’s conjecture
                              in dimension eight
                                                OpenAI

                                         September 26, 2026


                                                Abstract
         We resolve De Giorgi’s conjecture positively in dimension eight: every entire C 2 solution
     u : ℝ8 → (−1, 1) of Δu = u3 − u with an everywhere positive directional derivative is a planar
     heteroclinic. We prove that every stable solution v : ℝ7 → [−1, 1] of this equation is a constant
     well or a planar heteroclinic, without an energy-growth assumption.


1    Introduction
Consider the scalar Allen–Cahn equation

                                              Δu = u3 − u.                                               (1.1)

The function                                             √
                                           g(s) = tanh(s/ 2)
is its increasing one-dimensional transition from −1 to 1. Thus u(x) = g(e · x − c) solves (1.1)
whenever e is a unit vector; its level sets are parallel hyperplanes. De Giorgi’s conjecture asks
whether every bounded entire solution that is strictly increasing in a fixed direction has this form in
ambient dimension at most eight [6].
    The conjecture connects phase transitions with the Bernstein problem for minimal hypersurfaces.
For the quartic potential
                                                  1
                                         W (s) = (1 − s2 )2 ,
                                                  4
the rescaled phase-transition energies have surface tension times perimeter as their variational limit
[17, 19]. The graph condition in the minimal-surface problem is important: nonflat minimizing cones
occur in ambient dimension eight, whereas nonaffine entire minimal graphs first occur in ambient
dimension nine [3, 24]. The corresponding distinction between stable and monotone Allen–Cahn
solutions will also enter our proof.
    Theorem 1.2 has a geometric parallel in the stable Bernstein theorem for complete, connected,
two-sided stable smooth minimal immersions of boundaryless six-manifolds into ℝ7 : their images
are entire affine hyperplanes, with no volume-growth hypothesis [13, Theorem 1.1]. The Bernstein
theorem is not an input to our argument.
    Ghoussoub and Gui established the conjecture in dimension two [11], and Ambrosio and Cabré
in dimension three [2]. Savin proved the result through dimension eight under the additional
assumption
                    lim u(x′ , t) = −1,      lim u(x′ , t) = 1 for every x′ ∈ ℝn−1
                  t→−∞                       t→+∞



                                                    1
[21, Theorem 2.4]. These limits are pointwise in the transverse variable. Del Pino, Kowalczyk and
Wei constructed nonplanar strictly monotone solutions in every ambient dimension at least nine,
near large dilations of nonaffine minimal graphs [7]. The main result of this paper gives a positive
resolution in dimension eight without an assumption on the directional limits.

Theorem 1.1 (Monotone rigidity in dimension eight). Let u ∈ C 2 (ℝ8 , (−1, 1)) satisfy

                                 Δu = u3 − u,             ∂8 u > 0   in ℝ8 .

Then there are e ∈ 𝕊7 with e8 > 0 and c ∈ ℝ such that
                                             e·x−c
                                         (            )
                           u(x) = tanh         √              for every x ∈ ℝ8 .
                                                2
   Neither energy growth nor minimization is assumed. Those properties, and the well limits, will
be consequences of the argument. Both rigidity theorems concern the scalar quartic equation (1.1);
the monotonicity assumption in Theorem 1.1 is strict positivity of ∂8 u.

1.1   The stable equation
For an open set Ω ⊂ ℝn write
                                             ∫ (
                                                   1
                                                                      )
                                ℰ(v; Ω) =            |∇v|2 + W (v)        dx.
                                              Ω    2

A solution v is stable if its second variation satisfies
                           ∫
                                 |∇φ|2 + (3v 2 − 1)φ2 dx ≥ 0          for every φ ∈ Cc1 (ℝn ).
                               (                     )
               Qv (φ) :=                                                                         (1.2)
                           ℝn

A global minimizer minimizes ℰ in every ball among H 1 competitors with the same boundary trace.
Stability requires only the quadratic inequality, so it is a weaker condition than minimization.
   A strictly positive directional derivative solves the linearized equation and gives stability.
Moreover, the limits along that direction are stable solutions in one fewer variable. This observation
makes the following classification the analytic core of the paper.

Theorem 1.2 (Stable rigidity in dimension seven). Let v ∈ C 2 (ℝ7 , [−1, 1]) be a stable solution of
Δv = v 3 − v. Then either v ≡ 1, v ≡ −1, or
                                    a·x−b
                                    (             )
                        v(x) = tanh   √                    for some a ∈ 𝕊6 , b ∈ ℝ.
                                        2
No energy-growth assumption is required.

    This stability endpoint is sharp: Pacard and Wei constructed bounded stable nonplanar solutions
in dimension eight [20]. For the smooth quartic equation, Liu, Luo, Wang, Juncheng Wei, Yong Wei
and Wu prove stable rigidity in dimension three and monotone rigidity in dimension four [15]. The
same conclusions are obtained independently by Chan, Fernández-Real, Figalli, Florit-Simon and
Serra [4, Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2]. Florit-Simon and Serra prove stable rigidity in dimension
four under a bounded-energy-density hypothesis [10]. Both the higher dimension and the removal of
the density assumption matter here; they require different parts of the analysis.



                                                      2
1.2   From stable end profiles to the monotone theorem
We give the complete deduction of Theorem 1.1 from Theorem 1.2 at the beginning of the paper.
The comparison with competitors between the directional limits is due to Alberti, Ambrosio and
Cabré [1, Theorem 4.4]. Jerison and Monneau established the minimizing-end-profile reduction
[14, Theorem 1.3]. The positive derivative supplies stability, and the directional limits are stable
seven-dimensional solutions. Their classification allows constant wells or planar transitions at this
stage. Each is a global minimizer, by the one-dimensional calibration. Clipping a competitor
between the two end profiles and sliding translates of u then shows that u is itself a global minimizer.
Comparison with a constant phase inside a large ball gives ℰ(u; BR ) ≤ CR7 .
    The surface tension in this normalization is
                                  ∫ 1√               ∫               √
                                                          ′   2     2 2
                            s0 =        2W (t) dt =      g (s) ds =     .                          (1.3)
                                   −1                  ℝ             3

Under dilations u(Rj x) with Rj → ∞, the minimizing comparison identifies the entire limiting
energy measure with s0 times the perimeter measure of a minimizing boundary. This equality needs
a recovery sequence patched to the original solution; convergence of the phases alone would leave
open the possibility of extra energy.
    Energy monotonicity makes the limiting boundary a cone, that is, a boundary preserved by
dilations about the origin. Directional monotonicity fixes the sign of one component of its normal.
In dimension eight the minimizing-boundary regularity theory allows only a possible singularity at
the vertex [9]. The classical directed-cone argument then forces a hyperplane [22]. We include the
case in which that normal component vanishes identically: it gives translation invariance, which is
incompatible with an isolated singular vertex. The limiting density is therefore that of one planar
transition. Wang’s near-unit-density theorem [25, Theorem 11.1] returns to the original solution,
and the one-dimensional equation determines the profile and its orientation.
    This deduction explains why the stable theorem is the right target. It also separates the classical
geometric reduction from the new estimates needed for stable solutions. A reader interested primarily
in the conjecture can follow the deduction first; a reader interested in the stable classification can
proceed directly from its statement to the analytic argument in Part II. The classical gradient bound
and energy monotonicity needed by both paths are proved in Section 2.

1.3   The finite-density step
Let ωn−1 be the volume of the unit ball in ℝn−1 . For a solution in ℝn define

                                      ℰ(v; BR (y))
                           MR (y) =                ,    M∞ = sup MR (0).
                                      s0 ωn−1 Rn−1              R>0

A single planar transition has limiting density one. The first analytic step is the following result,
which is also needed locally in the argument that removes the density bound.

Theorem 1.3 (Finite-density stable rigidity). Let 4 ≤ n ≤ 7. Suppose v ∈ C 2 (ℝn , [−1, 1]) solves
Δv = v 3 − v and satisfies
                   ∫
                          |∇φ|2 + (3v 2 − 1)φ2 dx ≥ 0 for every φ ∈ Cc1 (ℝn ).
                        (                     )
                     ℝn

If M∞ < ∞, then v ≡ 1, v ≡ −1, or v(x) = g(a · x − b) for some a ∈ 𝕊n−1 and b ∈ ℝ.


                                                   3
    For stable solutions in ℝ7 , the remaining task is to prove that M∞ is finite. A penalized reach
measures both the bending of a zero sheet and its proximity to other sheets; the finite-density
theorem supplies local regularity at nearly this scale. Combining reach geometry with stability
bounds the fourth curvature moment and squared defect, controlling the regions where the zero set
is poorly approximated by planes. We then compare the reach inequality with an ambient stability
test built from the same radial amplitude on the sheets. The cost of joining neighboring amplitudes
is small enough to leave a strict positive margin, which contradicts the amount of surface area forced
by unbounded density.

1.4    Organization and conventions
Part I gives the complete deduction from stable end profiles to Theorem 1.1, conditional on
Theorem 1.2. Appendix A explains compatibility with the dimension-nine construction of del Pino,
Kowalczyk and Wei. After an optional guide to the analytical strategy in Section 4, Part II proves
the diffuse estimates, Theorem 1.3, and its local regularity consequence. Part III develops the reach
and interface estimates, joins the ambient and surface tests, and proves fourth-moment growth.
Part IV constructs the radial ambient test and completes the unbounded-density contradiction.
Thus the reading order places the geometric application first, while the analytic proof of the stable
theorem is contained in Parts II–IV. For a direct analytical reading, begin at Section 5, whose
opening recalls the equation, stability and all normalizations used there; the strategy section can be
read beforehand or consulted later.
    We write n = m + 1, so m is the interface dimension, and use dS for induced surface measure.
The surface tension s0 and the normal-average constant σ = 2s0 are kept distinct. All stability
tests are unrestricted compactly supported tests. Uniformity in translating centers, the order of
parameter choices, and successive limits are specified in the statements where they are needed.

2     Two classical estimates for the quartic equation
Both the geometric deduction and the analytical classification use the same gradient bound and
energy monotonicity. We record their short proofs here, independently of either rigidity theorem.
Let n = m + 1 ≥ 2 and let v ∈ C 2 (ℝn , [−1, 1]) solve Δv = v 3 − v. Throughout,
                                                                 √
                                          1       2 2           2 2
                              W (t) = (1 − t ) ,          s0 =      ,
                                          4                      3
and ωm denotes the volume of the unit ball in ℝm . Interior elliptic estimates bootstrap v to a
smooth function with uniform bounds for every derivative of fixed order.

2.1   Modica’s gradient estimate
We use Modica’s gradient bound [16],
                                          1
                                            |∇v|2 ≤ W (v).                                       (2.1)
                                          2
For completeness, its entire-solution argument in this normalization is short. For P = |∇v|2 /2−W (v),
the identities ∇P = ∇2 v∇v − W ′ (v)∇v and ΔP = |∇2 v|2 − W ′ (v)2 imply, wherever ∇v ̸= 0,

                                              2W ′ (v)
                                      ΔP ≥             ∇v · ∇P.
                                               |∇v|2
If sup P > 0, translate a maximizing sequence and pass to a locally smooth limit. Its P attains a
positive maximum. The strong maximum principle makes P constant on the component of {∇v ̸= 0}
containing that point. This component is also closed: on it |∇v|2 ≥ 2 sup P > 0. It is therefore all
of ℝn . The globally bounded smooth vector field ∇v has complete forward flow, and along that
flow v increases at rate at least 2 sup P , contradicting its boundedness. This proves (2.1).

2.2   Normalized energy and monotonicity
We recall Modica’s energy monotonicity formula [18] and give the stress-tensor calculation in the
present normalization. Set

                2                           s0 ωm rm Br (y)                  r>0

The stress tensor e(v)Id − ∇v ⊗ ∇v is divergence free. Its contraction with the derivative of x − y,
followed by the divergence theorem, gives
                        ∫                       ∫

The formula initially holds for almost every r and hence in integrated form for every pair of radii.
Containment of shifted balls shows that the limit at infinity is independent of y, and Mr (y) ≤ M∞ .
   The derivation uses neither stability nor a density-growth hypothesis.


Part I
The geometric deduction
3     From stable end profiles to the monotone theorem
In this part we deduce Theorem 1.1 from the unrestricted seven-dimensional classification in
Theorem 1.2, whose independent analytical proof occupies Parts II–IV. None of those parts uses the
geometric deduction given here. The end profiles will initially be allowed to be planar heteroclinics.
Their minimizing property suffices for the argument; no well limit is imposed on the given solution.
We first derive minimization and surface-order energy growth, then identify the complete limiting
energy with perimeter. Only after this exact identification do we use the geometry of a directed
minimizing cone and return to the equation at unit density.
   We use the energy ℰ and global minimizers defined in Section 1. It is enough to consider
competitors valued in [−1, 1]: truncation to this interval decreases both terms of the energy.
This definition also gives minimization in every bounded Lipschitz domain: extend a same-trace
competitor by u to a containing ball, use the Sobolev gluing property across the boundary, and
cancel the identical exterior energies. We retain the normalization
                               ∫ 1√               ∫               √
                                                      ′   2      2 2     σ
                          s0 =        2W (a) da =    g (s) ds =        = .                      (3.1)
                                −1                 ℝ               3     2

3.1   Stable end profiles and a variational comparison
The first task is to turn strict monotonicity into minimizing barriers. The positive directional
derivative gives stability, and a long-cylinder test transfers that stability to the seven-dimensional
limits. Their classification then supplies a calibration, even when a limit is a planar profile rather
than a constant well.
Lemma 3.1 (End profiles). Let u ∈ C 2 (ℝ8 , (−1, 1)) solve Δu = u3 − u and satisfy u8 > 0. There
are smooth stable solutions v− , v+ : ℝ7 → [−1, 1] such that
                   u(x′ , t + s) −→ v± (x′ )                k
                                                        in Cloc (ℝ8 )   as s → ±∞,      k ≥ 0.                  (3.2)
They satisfy v− (x′ ) < u(x′ , t) < v+ (x′ ) everywhere. Their extensions independent of the eighth
coordinate are global minimizers in ℝ8 .
Proof. Elliptic interior estimates, applied on unit balls, give smoothness and uniform bounds for
every fixed derivative order of u. The function h = u8 > 0 satisfies (−Δ + 3u2 − 1)h = 0. For every
compactly supported C 1 function η, integration by parts gives the ground-state identity
                          ∫                            ∫       ⏐ ( )⏐2
                                                                  η ⏐⏐
                                |∇η|2 + (3u2 − 1)η 2 =     h2 ⏐⏐∇
                              (                     )          ⏐
                                                                       ≥ 0.                    (3.3)
                           ℝ8                           ℝ8        h ⏐

                                                                6
The division is legitimate because h has a positive minimum on the support of η.
    Bounded monotonicity gives pointwise limits along each vertical line. Translation compactness
and the derivative bounds improve this to (3.2). Indeed every convergent subsequence has the same
pointwise limit, and a bounded vertical shift leaves that limit unchanged. Passing the equation and
stability to these limits first gives solutions and stability in eight dimensions. To see stability in

Letting L → ∞ proves the assertion. For example, u(x′ , t) < u(x′ , t + 1) ≤ v+ (x′ ), which proves the
strict upper inequality; the lower one is analogous.
    Theorem 1.2 says that each v± is a constant
                                             ∫ a √ well or a planar heteroclinic. To check the
minimizing assertion directly, put Φ(a) = −1 2W (b) db. For a unit vector e and a competitor
w ∈ H 1 (Ω, [−1, 1]),
                             1                   √
                               |∇w|2 + W (w) ≥ 2W (w) ∂e w = ∂e Φ(w).                             (3.4)
                             2
There is equality when w(x) = g(e · x − c), since g ′ = 2W (g). The integral of the last expression
                                                       √

depends only on the boundary trace. Thus such a profile minimizes on every ball, in any ambient
dimension in which e is a unit vector. This includes the extensions of the seven-dimensional profiles,
with e8 = 0. The constants ±1 have zero energy and also minimize.

    The next comparison also applies in other dimensions. It records exactly which property of
the end profiles is used. The constrained comparison between end profiles was proved by Alberti,
Ambrosio and Cabré [1, Theorem 4.4]. The unrestricted minimization implication is the classical
theorem of Jerison and Monneau [14, Theorem 1.3, p. 441; proof on pp. 452–453]; we include the
compact comparison argument in the present normalization. Its globally C 1,1 potential hypothesis
is met by                                ⎧
                                                 2
                                         ⎨(s + 1) , s < −1,
                                         ⎪
                                         ⎪
                                 W̃ (s) = W (s),     −1 ≤ s ≤ 1,
                                         ⎪
                                         ⎩(s − 1)2 , s > 1.
                                         ⎪

The values and the first two derivatives match at the wells, and W̃ ′ is globally Lipschitz. Both
potentials are nonnegative and agree on [−1, 1]. Clipping any competitor to this interval decreases
its gradient energy and replaces its exterior potential by zero for either potential, so their minimizing
comparisons for u agree.
Lemma 3.2 (Minimizing barriers and compact sliding). Let N ≥ 2, and suppose a bounded solution
u : ℝN −1 × ℝ → (−1, 1) of Δu = W ′ (u) is strictly increasing in the last coordinate. Suppose
its vertical limits a± (x′ ), extended to ℝN , are global minimizers. Then u is a global minimizer.
Moreover there is a dimensional constant CN such that

                           ℰ(u; BR (y)) ≤ CN RN −1        (y ∈ ℝN , R ≥ 2).                         (3.5)

Proof. Fix a ball B. The direct method produces an energy minimizer w with boundary trace u;
truncation allows |w| ≤ 1. First clip it from below by a− , writing w1 = max{w, a− }. The Sobolev
lattice identity is

                   ℰ(max{w, a− }; B) + ℰ(min{w, a− }; B) = ℰ(w; B) + ℰ(a− ; B).

                                                   7
Since u > a− on ∂B, the minimum on the left has the same trace as a− . Minimality of a− therefore
shows ℰ(w1 ; B) ≤ ℰ(w; B). The maximum has trace u, so it remains an unconstrained minimizer.
The analogous upper clipping gives another unconstrained minimizer w̃ = min{w1 , a+ } satisfying
a− ≤ w̃ ≤ a+ .
    The Euler–Lagrange equation and elliptic boundary regularity make w̃ smooth up to ∂B. A
difference of two solutions b, c here satisfies −Δ(b − c) + (b2 + bc + c2 − 1)(b − c) = 0, with bounded
zeroth-order coefficient. Strong comparison for a nonnegative solution of this linear equation,
together with the strict boundary inequalities, gives

                                           a− < w̃ < a+         on B.

These gaps have positive minima on this compact set. Vertical translates us (x′ , t) = u(x′ , t + s)
converge uniformly there to a+ as s → +∞, so us > w̃ for all sufficiently large s. Let

                                      s∗ = inf{s ≥ 0 : us ≥ w̃ on B}.

The set inside the infimum is an upper interval, by strict monotonicity. Continuity gives us∗ ≥ w̃.
If s∗ > 0, then us∗ > u = w̃ on ∂B, and strong comparison gives strict inequality throughout B.
Compactness and continuity then allow a smaller positive translation, a contradiction. Thus w̃ ≤ u.
For the reverse inequality, u−s lies below w̃ when s is large. Decrease s ≥ 0 until first contact.
A positive first-contact parameter is again impossible, because u−s < u = w̃ on ∂B and strong
comparison gives a strict compact gap. At s = 0 this yields u ≤ w̃. Consequently u = w̃ on B, and
B was arbitrary.
    Finally choose a smooth cutoff ηR that is zero on BR−1 (y), one near ∂BR (y), and satisfies
|∇ηR | ≤ C. The competitor 1 + ηR (u − 1) has zero energy in the inner ball and bounded energy
density in the remaining shell. The latter assertion uses |u| ≤ 1 and the uniform unit-scale interior
gradient estimate for the equation. The shell has volume at most CN RN −1 , proving (3.5).

   Lemmas 3.1 and 3.2 apply to the solution of Theorem 1.1. They establish minimization and the
required energy growth directly from its hypotheses and Theorem 1.2.

3.2   Exact convergence to a minimizing boundary
We have obtained the variational comparison needed for the blow-down. The remaining issue in this
step is equality of measures: phase convergence and a perimeter lower bound alone would permit
additional diffuse energy. We eliminate that possibility by comparing with a recovery sequence
patched to the original solution in a thin annular layer. This argument does not require a prescribed
trace for the recovery sequence.
    We use the following established variational and geometric inputs. For a finite-perimeter set F
in a bounded Lipschitz domain Ω, the Modica–Mortola recovery theorem provides, for any sequence
εj ↓ 0, functions wj ∈ H 1 (Ω, [−1, 1]) such that

                                                         wj −→ 2𝟏F − 1            in L1 (Ω),
                               ∫ (                          )
                                     εj           W (wj )                                        (3.6)
                     lim sup            |∇wj |2 +               ≤ s0 P (F ; Ω).
                        j      Ω     2              εj

Here P (F ; Ω) = |D𝟏F |(Ω) is relative perimeter [19]; the finite-perimeter recovery statement is
also proved in Modica [17, Section 3, pp. 140–141]. For a locally perimeter-minimizing set in ℝN ,
its perimeter measure defines a stationary integral varifold with multiplicity one on the reduced


                                                     8
boundary. Its support is a smooth embedded minimal hypersurface outside a closed singular set of
Hausdorff dimension at most N − 8 (and the singular set is empty when N ≤ 7). The centered area
ratio is nondecreasing, and equality at all scales forces a cone. These are the classical regularity,
dimension-reduction, and monotonicity results for minimizing boundaries [8, 12, 24]; for the sharp
singular-set dimension bound, see Federer [9, Theorem 1, p. 769]. The argument below uses recovery
and comparison to prove exact energy convergence before using this multiplicity-one geometric
theory.
Proposition 3.3 (Exact perimeter limit). Let u : ℝN → [−1, 1] be an entire global minimizer with
ℰ(u; BR ) ≤ CRN −1 for R ≥ 2. Given Rj → ∞, set εj = Rj−1 and uj (x) = u(Rj x). After taking a
subsequence there is a locally finite-perimeter set E such that

                                                 uj −→ 2𝟏E − 1 in L1loc (ℝN ),
                  (                          )
                      εj           W (uj )                                                            (3.7)
                         |∇uj |2 +               dx ⇀ s0 |D𝟏E |         as local Radon measures.
                      2             εj

The set E is locally perimeter minimizing against compact perturbations.
                                                                                  ∫a √
Proof. Compactness and the lower bound. Recall the phase primitive Φ(a) = −1 2W (b) db.
Denote the rescaled energy by ℰj and its energy measure by μj . Scaling the minimizing comparison
shows that uj minimizes ℰj against every compact modification. A change of variables gives

                                         μj (Br ) = Rj1−N ℰ(u; BRj r ).                               (3.8)

Thus these measures are locally bounded. Moreover
                                                                    ∫

                   |∇Φ(uj )| ≤       |∇uj |2 +         ,                W (uj ) ≤ εj μj (K) −→ 0

on each compact set K. Local BV compactness, followed by a diagonal subsequence, implies that
Φ(uj ) has an L1loc limit. The vanishing potential forces its values to lie in {Φ(−1), Φ(1)} = {0, s0 }.
The inverse of Φ is uniformly continuous on [0, s0 ], so this also proves the asserted local L1 convergence
of uj to 2𝟏E − 1. Weak compactness of μj and lower semicontinuity of total variation give a local
measure limit μ satisfying
                                             μ ≥ s0 |D𝟏E |.                                            (3.9)
    A recovery comparison with the correct boundary trace. We prove the opposite inequality,
including the boundary gluing. Fix a ball Br (y) and δ > 0. Let F be a finite-perimeter set in
Br+δ (y) that agrees with E on the annulus A = Br+δ (y) \ Br (y). The case F = E is permitted.
Choose a recovery sequence wj on this larger ball as in (3.6). Both wj and uj converge on A to

   Use wj inside Sj , the interpolation on Sj , and uj outside Sj . This is an admissible compact
modification of uj . Its minimizing property, and nonnegativity of the original energy in the part of
the annulus inside Sj , give

                              ℰj (uj ; Br (y)) ≤ ℰj (wj ; Br+δ (y)) + oj (1).

Consequently, whenever μ(∂Br (y)) = 0,

                                    μ(Br (y)) ≤ s0 P (F ; Br+δ (y)).                           (3.13)

Equality of energy and perimeter, then minimality. First set F = E. At radii with μ(∂Br (y)) =
|D𝟏E |(∂Br (y)) = 0, send δ ↓ 0 in (3.13). These are all but countably many radii at each center.
Together with (3.9), the resulting inequalities on balls prove μ = s0 |D𝟏E | as measures. More
explicitly, μ − s0 |D𝟏E | is already a nonnegative Radon measure by (3.9), and it vanishes on these
balls, which form a neighborhood basis. In particular no additional transition-layer mass remains.
    Next suppose F △ E is compactly contained in an open ball. Choose r outside that compact
set, inside the ball, and with zero perimeter measure on ∂Br (y). Then F = E near this sphere,
so it is a continuity sphere for both perimeter measures. Apply (3.13) and let δ ↓ 0 to obtain
P (E; Br (y)) ≤ P (F ; Br (y)). The perimeter measures coincide outside the compact modification;
hence this proves local perimeter minimality, as asserted.

3.3   The directed minimizing cone in dimension eight
The exact limit is now available together with perimeter minimality. We next combine energy
monotonicity with vertical monotonicity: the first makes the limiting boundary a cone, while the
second fixes the sign of its vertical normal component. In this geometric step, dimension eight enters
when we analyze the singular set of this cone.
    For our eight-dimensional solution, the gradient inequality (2.1) and monotonicity formula (2.3)
give a nondecreasing normalized energy

                                                     ℰ(u; Br )
                                          Mr (u) =             .
                                                      s0 ω7 r7
By (3.5) it has a finite limit M∞ . This limit is positive, since u is nonconstant and its energy is
positive on some ball. Apply Proposition 3.3 to any sequence Rj → ∞. At continuity radii, its
exact convergence and (3.8) imply

                                         P (E; Br ) = ω7 r7 M∞ .                               (3.14)

                                                    10
Squeezing between continuity radii proves this identity for every r > 0. Thus the stationary varifold
of the minimizing boundary has constant centered area ratio. The equality case of monotonicity
makes its support
                                         C = supp |D𝟏E |
a cone with vertex at the origin. It is nonempty and contains the origin. Its multiplicity is one,
since its weight is the perimeter measure.
    Vertical monotonicity also survives this convergence. For each nonnegative ψ ∈ Cc1 (ℝ8 ),
                                     ∫            ∫
                                   − uj ∂8 ψ = (∂8 uj )ψ ≥ 0.

The L1 limit gives ∂8 𝟏E ≥ 0 as a distribution and hence as a measure. Let νE denote the unit
normal pointing into E on the regular boundary, so that D𝟏E = νE |D𝟏E |. Then

                               νE · e8 ≥ 0   on the regular part of C.                         (3.15)

Lemma 3.4 (Flatness of the directed cone). Let E ⊂ ℝ8 be locally perimeter minimizing. Suppose
its nonempty boundary support C is a cone with vertex 0 and ∂8 𝟏E ≥ 0. Then C is a hyperplane,
with perimeter multiplicity one.

Proof. This is the classical directed-cone reduction; see also Schnürer [22, Theorem 4.5 and Corol-
lary 4.6]. We give its short proof to make the treatment of the vertex explicit. The cone has a smooth
compact link. The singular-set dimension bound is at most zero. A nonzero singular point would,
by dilation invariance, generate a whole singular ray: each dilation is an ambient diffeomorphism
preserving C, and therefore preserves the distinction between smooth and singular boundary points.
This is impossible, so Sing C ⊂ {0}. Consequently

                                             Λ = C ∩ 𝕊7

is a smooth compact embedded minimal hypersurface of 𝕊7 , possibly with several connected
components. The oriented normal is continuous on the regular boundary. Since tangent normal
lines are dilation invariant and the choice of side is continuous, that orientation is constant along
every regular ray: it takes values in the same two-point set along that connected ray. This uses
conicality of the support and does not presume conicality of E. The function f = νE · e8 ≥ 0 is
therefore homogeneous of degree zero.
    The normal component of a Euclidean translation on a minimal hypersurface satisfies the Jacobi
equation [24, Corollary 3.3.1, p. 74]

                                        ΔC f + |AC |2 f = 0.

The cone metric is dr2 + r2 gΛ and |AC |2 = r−2 |AΛ |2 , where AΛ is the second fundamental form of
Λ in the sphere. Thus
                                        ΔΛ f + |AΛ |2 f = 0.                                  (3.16)
We separate the case where this nonnegative function is positive on a link component from the case
where it vanishes on every component. A positive directional normal component. On a connected
component where f is not identically zero, the∫ strong maximum principle gives f > 0. Integration
of (3.16) on that compact component yields |AΛ |2 f = 0, so it is totally geodesic. A connected
compact embedded totally geodesic hypersurface of the sphere is a whole equatorial 𝕊6 . Any
other component, being disjoint from this equator, would lie in one of its open hemispheres. This

                                                 11
cannot happen: the corresponding signed coordinate function ℓ on a closed minimal six-dimensional
hypersurface of 𝕊7 satisfies ΔΛ ℓ + 6ℓ = 0, hence has integral zero, whereas in an open hemisphere it
has strict constant sign. There are consequently no other components, and C is the hyperplane over
that equator.
   A vanishing directional normal component. It remains to handle the case f ≡ 0 on all of Λ.
Perimeter measure gives zero mass to the possible singular point, so now
                                      ∂ 8 𝟏E = 0   on all of ℝ8 .                               (3.17)
A locally integrable function whose distributional derivative in one direction vanishes is invariant
under translations in that direction. For example this follows by mollifying, applying the assertion
to the smooth functions, and passing to the local L1 limit. Thus each vertical translation preserves
𝟏E as an L1loc class, and also preserves its perimeter measure, its support C, and the singular set of
that support. If 0 were singular, every point of the vertical axis would be singular, contradicting
Sing C ⊂ {0}. Therefore the origin is regular. A cone smooth at its vertex equals its tangent
hyperplane there: dilating toward the origin keeps the cone fixed while its smooth local graphs
converge to that tangent plane. This proves flatness in the remaining case. In either case the weight
is ℋ7 ⌞ C, by the multiplicity-one structure of a minimizing boundary.

   Applying Lemma 3.4 to (3.14) shows
                                            M∞ (u) = 1.                                         (3.18)
This is the point where the exact measure convergence in Proposition 3.3 is needed: flat support
alone would not determine the diffuse energy density.

3.4   Return to the equation and the exact profile
The near-unit-density theorem of Wang [25, Theorem 11.1, p. 3036] says that, in every ambient
dimension N , a nonconstant bounded entire Allen–Cahn solution with the Modica bound and
                                           ℰ(u; BR )
                                     lim               ≤ 1 + δN
                                    R→∞ s0 ωN −1 RN −1

for a sufficiently small dimensional δN > 0 is one-dimensional. The constant wells can be included
as the separate trivial alternatives; see also Florit-Simon and Serra [10, Theorem 2.16]. Our solution
has the required range and Modica bound, is nonconstant by u8 > 0, and satisfies this energy
condition by (3.18). Hence u(x) = h(e · x) for some unit vector e.
    For completeness we recover the profile and its orientation directly. Since u8 = e8 h′ > 0, we
have e8 ̸= 0; reversing e and the scalar coordinate if necessary gives e8 > 0 and h′ > 0 everywhere.
The finite limits a± = lims→±∞ h(s) are distinct and lie in [−1, 1]. Translated compactness for
the ODE h′′ = h3 − h shows a3± − a± = 0 and h′ (s) → 0 at both ends. Indeed any translated
subsequential limit at an end is the constant a± , and the uniform derivative bounds give convergence
of its derivatives. The first integral is
                                   1 ′ 2
                                     h (s) − W (h(s)) = constant.
                                   2
Taking both limits gives W (a− ) = W (a+ ). Among the three stationary roots −1, 0, 1, the only
distinct pair with equal potential is −1, 1. Thus a− = −1, a+ = 1, and the first integral and h′ > 0
give
                                     1 − h2                   s−c
                                                            (      )
                               h′ = √ ,          h(s) = tanh √
                                        2                       2

                                                   12
for some c ∈ ℝ. This proves Theorem 1.1, including the condition e8 > 0. Conversely this formula
has strict range (−1, 1), satisfies g ′′ = g 3 − g, and obeys u8 = e8 g ′ (e · x − c) > 0.


Part II
Stable solutions with finite density
4     Guide to the analytical proof
This section explains how the three analytical parts fit together. It may be skipped on a first reading:
the definitions and proofs begin afresh in Section 5.

4.1   Finite density and the diffuse defect
The main quantities measure departure from a planar transition. For |v| < 1, put

                          q = 1 − v2,        U = g −1 (v),       D = 1 − |∇U |2 .

Modica’s gradient bound gives D ≥ 0 [16]. A planar transition has affine U and D = 0. For a stable
solution the amplitude q gives the exact identity
                                ∫                 ∫
                 0 ≤ Qv (qη) =       2     2
                                    q |∇η| dx −     q 3 Dη 2 dx    (η ∈ Cc1 (ℝn )).          (4.1)
                                  ℝn                        ℝn

The inverse-profile formulation and this test also occur in Liu et al. [15, Lemma 2.1]; we derive
them in the present dimension. The second term detects defect, including at zeros where a smooth
level-set description is unavailable.
    At a regular point of Σ = {v = 0}, let ν = ∇U/|∇U | be the normal pointing toward increasing
v, let A = dν|T Σ be the second fundamental form, and set H = tr A. Small defect gives a local
description by one nearly planar transition. For its normal averages the appropriate normalization is
                                                    ∫              √
                                        2                  2      4 2
                         G(s) = 1 − g(s) ,    σ=       G(s) ds =       = 2s0 .
                                                     ℝ              3

For a fixed, sufficiently large normal length P , define
                                           ∫ P
                                       1
                            IP (z) =             q(z + sν(z))3 D(z + sν(z)) ds.                   (4.2)
                                       σ    −P

This is an average along a normal segment, not the pointwise defect D(z). We prove that IP controls
both curvature and mean curvature, with a sharp coefficient in the latter bound. The limiting
equation obtained by normalizing D by its value D(zj ) → 0 at a zero has a nonnegative source even
in the normal coordinate. Separating this source from the positive homogeneous solutions gives the
strict coefficient needed when the interface has dimension six.
    For the later unbounded-density argument in dimension seven, we will also need a surface
inequality. Lifting a surface test into the ambient equation yields
                                     ∫                    ∫
                                         |A|2 + IP 2
                             (1 − ε)              ψ dS ≤     |∇Σ ψ|2 dS.                      (4.3)
                                       Σ     2             Σ



                                                       13
Here P is chosen first, sufficiently large depending on ε > 0, and ψ is Lipschitz and compactly
supported where D < δ, with δ then sufficiently small. The two favorable terms come from normal
tube geometry and profile defect, respectively. When interaction dominates curvature, this inequality
retains only about half of the mass IP . The final ambient test will retain almost all of that mass for
the specifically chosen radial amplitude. This improvement is what makes the eventual comparison
in dimension seven decisive.
    Returning to finite density, we use the preliminary critical-density and regularity results of
Florit-Simon and Serra [10, Sections 3–5]. These results apply through ambient dimension seven;
their final classification theorem is four-dimensional. They reduce a possible failure of Theorem 1.3
to an integer-density solution whose zero set consists, away from a sparse set, of ordered sheets. The
transition-layer estimates of Wang and Wei [26] apply after establishing the required nonvanishing
core gradient and a bound on the full ambient derivative of its direction. We keep these hypotheses
explicit.
    The remaining contradiction compares the nearest gap between sheets in two ways. A preliminary
concave test controls the geometric errors. A sharper gap test then combines the mean-curvature
bound with a radial Green estimate, while diffuse stability supplies the competing Hardy bound.
Their logarithmic coefficients are incompatible through dimension seven. The resulting finite-density
theorem makes every fixed finite density threshold available in the local regularity theorem used
later.

4.2    Geometry without a global density bound
The remaining task is to exclude unbounded density for a stable solution in ℝ7 . The relevant
geometry can first be seen in an ordinary normal tube around a smooth sheet: its points have the
form z + tν(z), where ν is the unit normal. Such a tube can cease to describe one isolated transition
because the sheet bends or because a second sheet enters it. Both obstructions must be measured
when the number of sheets is not known in advance.
   For two different zeros z, y, with z regular, the ratio

                                                |y − z|2
                                             |ν(z) · (y − z)|
is twice the radius of the normal tangent ball through y. When the denominator vanishes, the ratio
is interpreted as +∞ and there is no such finite ball. As y approaches z, it also records curvature.
We use a smoothed angular penalty to define a comparable length d(z), called the penalized reach.
Its purpose is to retain these two geometric controls while allowing a differential comparison at a
second contact point. The penalty and its precise properties are introduced where this comparison
is proved.
     A delayed energy-monotonicity argument finds a bounded-density window at nearly the reach
scale. The preceding local regularity theorem then isolates a single sheet there, although the original
solution has no global density bound. Projection onto the translation mode of g improves the
mean-curvature derivatives. A tangent ball on one phase side controls signed mean curvature at a
remote contact. These estimates produce a differential inequality for d and a change-of-variables
formula that counts its positive curvature contributions with multiplicity one.
     We next connect this local geometry to stability. Tests lifted from a sheet use the derivative of the
one-dimensional profile. Where several lifts reach the same ambient point, their squares are added
before taking a square root. The resulting gradient identity retains a nonnegative variance term.
Joining these tests to q near zeros with large defect preserves positive defect mass and controls the
cost of the joining region. Combining the resulting inequality with the Simons–Schoen–Simon–Yau

                                                   14
curvature method [23, 24] yields a fourth-moment growth bound. Interaction is measured here by
E = e−κd , where κ > 0 is fixed by the reach penalty. The estimate controls weighted integrals
of |A|4 + D2 + E 2 , together with the weighted count of a separated net of large-defect zeros, by
K(R)Rm−4 , where m = 6 and K(R) = 1 + supx MR (x). This gains four powers of R over the
corresponding weighted area bound, even though K(R) may grow. The mean curvature need not
vanish, so its derivative terms remain part of this calculation.
     This passage from ambient stability to the transition set is related to the free-boundary work of
Chan, Fernández-Real, Figalli and Serra [5] and to Liu et al. [15, Sections 3 and 5] for the smooth
quartic equation in dimension three. The latter joins 1 − v 2 to transition-profile derivatives and
uses a surface inequality, Gauss–Bonnet, and logarithmic cutoffs. Here the six-dimensional interface
requires the reach inequality, the fourth-moment growth estimate, and the final radial comparison.
The two joining operations also have different purposes: the first changes from ambient to surface
tests near large-defect zeros; the final one interpolates amplitudes between neighboring sheets.
     In that final comparison, the same radial reach weight is used in the geometric inequality and the
ambient stability test. The growth estimate controls the regions where the regular-sheet description
fails. On the remaining regions, the surface amplitude is extended normally and interpolated
between adjacent sheets in fixed-width bands inside their logarithmic gaps. The cost is proportional
to the squared difference of the two amplitudes, so it vanishes when they agree. Assign each portion
of a switching band to the base with the larger uncut amplitude. On that base’s nearest gap, the
reach comparison makes the amplitude ratio tend to one, so the normalized loss vanishes. Of the
two possible sides at the assigned base, only the other side can therefore
                                                                   ∫         incur the fixed positive loss.
The   resulting  stability estimate retains almost nine  tenths of    I  ψ 2 dS, while the coefficient of
∫                                                                   Σ P
           2 dS stays close to one. For the same radial amplitude ψ, this is stronger in the interaction
  Σ |∇ Σ ψ|
term than (4.3) and provides the additional control of IP needed in the reach comparison. Expanding
the shared radial gradient and comparing its coefficients with the reach inequality leaves a strict
margin in interface dimension six. A normal-tube packing argument then converts the estimate into
an area contradiction, excluding unbounded density and completing Theorem 1.2.


5    Diffuse fields and a sharp mean-curvature comparison
This section begins the analytical proof of stable rigidity. It uses the classical estimates of Section 2
and can be read independently of the geometric deduction in Part I. Its aim is to bound the mean
curvature of a zero layer by the defect from a planar transition, with the strict coefficient needed for
finite-density rigidity. The argument has three steps: an exact stability identity, estimates uniform
after division by a small defect, and a classification of the positive functions arising in the normalized
limit.
    Throughout this section the ambient dimension n ≥ 2 is fixed. All constants are uniform over
entire solutions v ∈ C 2 (ℝn , [−1, 1]) of

5.3   Zeros and decay inside either phase
The weighted identity (5.8) will eventually be compared with integrals over Σ = {v = 0}. We
therefore need to know that Σ exists, and that the weight q becomes exponentially small far from
it. The following elementary comparison proves both facts; its open-set version will also apply to
individual chambers between zero layers.

                 √ inside a phase). A solution with |v| < 1 has a nonempty zero set Σ = {v = 0}.
Lemma 5.2 (Clearing
For every 0 < γ < 2 there is Cγ = C(n, γ) such that
                                                       √
                                         q(x) ≤ Cγ e−( 2−γ) dist(x,Σ) .                                  (5.14)

More generally the same estimate, with dist(x, ∂Ω) in place of dist(x, Σ), holds for any solution in
an open set Ω with 0 < v ≤ 1 there, and also with the signs reversed.

Proof. Clearing on large balls. Let ϕR be the positive first Dirichlet eigenfunction of a ball BR (x),

5.5    Separating the even source from the normal derivative
In the limit just obtained, the source 2G|𝒯 |2 is even in the normal coordinate. We use that symmetry
to remove its contribution to Fs (0) without discarding its nonnegative mass. The next lemma treats
the inhomogeneous source explicitly. No bound on its growth at infinity is assumed. The hypothesis
that a nonnegative solution F exists supplies the local bounds needed in the exhaustion.

    The even inhomogeneous part. For each integer R solve ℒ0 ER = S on BR (0) with zero Dirichlet

an uncontrolled source integral with a limit.

b = 0 the full-line ratio equals 1/(2 2), and any finite truncation strictly decreases its positive
denominator. In particular a finite-window proof cannot retain the exact endpoint constant merely
by invoking local convergence.

6    Rigidity under a finite density bound
We now prove Theorem 1.3, in its full range 4 ≤ n ≤ 7. This will also make a density bound on one
sufficiently large ball a regularity criterion; that local consequence is the input for the later analysis
without a global density
                    √       bound. Write n = m + 1, let ωm be the volume of the unit ball in ℝm , and
keep s0 = σ/2 = 2 2/3. Recall the normalized energy
                               ∫
                         1                              1
           Mr (y) =          m
                                       e(v),    e(v) = |∇v|2 + W (v),        M∞ = sup Mr (0),
                     s0 ωm r    Br (y)                  2                            r>0

as defined in (2.2).
    The statements from the literature used below are preliminary results of Florit-Simon–Serra,
with their numbering fixed to [10], version 1. Their final classification theorem concerns n = 4; the
extension to n = 5, 6, 7 here is the gap argument following Lemma 6.1.
    Suppose the theorem fails. The literature reduction produces a critical solution with an integer
number K ≥ 2 of zero layers outside a sparse set. We will compare two estimates for the nearest
gap between those layers. A Green test bounds its average in terms of mean curvature; Lemma 5.6
and a Hardy test bound that curvature cost. The opposite orientations of adjacent transitions force
almost all gaps to be logarithmically large. A preliminary square-root gap test is needed before the
sharper, nearly linear test: it makes the gradient error in the gap Laplacian absorbable without
losing the final coefficient.


                                                         23
6.1   Reduction to a critical integer-density solution
By (2.3), Mr (y) is nondecreasing and its limit at infinity is independent of y. In the diffuse variables,
the discrepancy in that formula is q 2 D/4 by (5.7); thus
                                     {        ∫                    ∫                }

We will use its two nonnegative terms separately. The derivation in Section 2 uses neither stability
nor finite density; finite density now enters through the following reduction.
   A number Kf > 0 is called subcritical in dimension n if the conclusion of Theorem 1.3 holds for
every bounded entire stable solution with M∞ ≤ Kf . The range of a bounded entire solution is
automatically contained in [−1, 1], by applying the equation to translated limits at its supremum
and infimum. The constants ±1 are included in this definition.

The two regularity inputs. There are two different ways to obtain a graphical region: subcritical
energy density, or small local generalized-curvature mass. We state both hypotheses because the
critical solution uses the second before the theorem makes the first available at every finite threshold.
For a regular point put 𝒜v = |∇(∇v/|∇v|)|, where the derivative is the full ambient derivative. The
sheeting assumptions on Br are

                          |∇v| ≥ C −1 ,     𝒜v ≤ C/r     on Br ∩ {|v| ≤ 0.9}.

Thus a tangential curvature bound alone would not be this hypothesis. For entire stable parameter-
one solutions in n ≤ 7, [10, Theorem 3.2] gives these assumptions on Bδr when Kf is subcritical,
Mr ≤ Kf + δ, and r−1 ≤ δ; δ > 0, C < ∞ depend on Kf , n. The separate good-ball result [10,
Theorem 3.4] assumes Mr ≤ C0 , r ≥ r0 (C0 , n), and
                      ∫
                             𝒜2v |∇v|2 < δbad for every x ∈ Br ∩ {|v| ≤ 0.9}.
                         B1 (x)

It gives the sheeting assumptions on Br/2 , with constants depending on C0 , n. In this integral the
curvature density is defined almost everywhere as 𝒜2v |∇v|2 = |∇2 v|2 − |∇|∇v||2 .

What sheeting supplies. Under the sheeting assumptions, [10, Lemma 2.13 and Theorem 2.14]
give graphical decomposition and the Wang–Wei estimates [26]; the latter permit n ≤ 10. Theorem 1.1
of the final Wang–Wei author manuscript, dated 9 October 2019, uses a potential with second
derivative one at the wells. Our normalization is transferred to theirs by

                        Ŵ = W/2,      v̂(y) = v(y/ 2),      Δy v̂ = Ŵ ′ (v̂).

Then
√      Ŵ ′′ (±1) = 1, stability is preserved by change of variables, and lengths are multiplied by
  2. This fixed rescaling changes only the constants in the estimates below; the parameter-one
formulation in [10, Theorem 2.14] already uses our quartic normalization. On smaller cylinders they
give scaled C 2,θ bounds, mean curvature O(r−2 ), and separation tending to infinity as r → ∞. We
will only need the weaker Cι r−2+ι mean-curvature bound for each fixed ι > 0. When all zeros in the
original ball lie in a sufficiently thin slab, [10, Theorem 4.8 and Lemmas 4.9–4.10] give arbitrarily
small slopes, comparison of density with the integer number of graphs, and local approximation by
a planar heteroclinic on every fixed window. Interior elliptic estimates upgrade that approximation
to smooth local convergence. The thickness threshold, the interior fractions, and the constants are
uniform once the sheeting constants, dimension, and desired accuracy have been fixed.

Proof. We first obtain the critical solution and uniform information at its bad centers. We then
transfer that information to every distant good zero, where the later gap test will be applied.
    Attainment and the bad set. Near-unit-density rigidity, stated in [10, Theorem 2.16] and
originating in [25, Theorem 11.1], makes 1 + δ(n) subcritical. If some finite density is not subcritical,
the finite critical density is attained by [10, Proposition 3.6]. Take the bad set in its Definition 3.7:
its centers satisfy |v| ≤ 0.9 and the reverse nonstrict inequality in the good-ball curvature condition
above. Lemma 3.8 there makes this set nonempty. It is harmless to take its closure; all estimates
below pass to that closure by continuity and shifted-ball containment. Propositions 3.9–3.10
there give uniform large-scale planar confinement, uniform convergence of density at these centers,
integrality of the critical density, and the no-gaps conclusion. Thus K ≥ 2. Lemma 5.1 and
Proposition 5.2 there give the asserted sparse bad-set estimate. These results explicitly assume
n ≤ 7, including Section 5 of that paper.
    For clarity, the enlargement from 1 to a fixed h costs only a constant depending on h, n. Choose
a maximal separated family of bad centers in Br+h+2 (y) with fixed small separation. Disjoint balls
of a still smaller fixed radius about its centers lie in B1 (Z) ∩ Br+h+3 (y). Balls of radius h + 2 about
this family cover Bh (Z) ∩ Br (y). This proves the claim using the estimate at the slightly larger
radius.

6.2   Surface measures and radial estimates
We first transfer the ambient energy and stability bounds to the regular zero surface. Four estimates
will be used: defect mass grows at most like rm−2 , radial geometric errors have a vanishing tail,
surface mass retains the coefficient K, and a Hardy test fixes the sharp coefficient of the logarithmic
defect integral. All radial tests are centered at arbitrary points of Z.
Lemma 6.2 (Tube, mass and Hardy estimates). For any prescribed finite P , increasing S makes
                               Φ(z, t) = z + tν(z),                z ∈ ΣS/2 ,   |t| ≤ P,

Proof. We prove the assertions in their order: first tube injectivity and defect mass, then the radial
tail, then the surface mass retained outside the bad set, and finally the Hardy coefficient.
    Tube injectivity and defect mass. Curvature O(1/s) and profile convergence give the local tube
description. To verify injectivity, a collision would have base points within distance 2P , hence in
a common good chart when S is large. Different sheets cannot collide because their separation
tends to infinity; on a single sheet the small slope and curvature give uniqueness of the normal
projection on this fixed-width tube. The volume Jacobian is det(Id + tA) = 1 + oS (1). A unit
tube, on which q is bounded below, controls surface area by energy. Bounded density therefore
gives the area bound for arbitrary centers. Local Harnack comparison in (5.9) also gives the first
inequality of (6.2). To obtain its second inequality, apply (5.8) with a cutoff equal to one on Br+1 (x),
supported in B2r+2 (x), and with gradient bounded by C/r; use q 2 ≤ 4e(v). On good zeros the
identity H = ∂ν D/(2|p|2 ) gives |H| ≤ CD.
    The radial error has a vanishing tail. Integrating the second term in (2.3) gives

6.3   Nearest-sheet distance and its weak Laplacian
The mass estimates are now available. To use them in a Green test, we need an upper Laplacian
bound for the distance to a neighboring sheet and an exact count of how often a target point can
receive a curvature term. The following argument works with local adjacent sheets and does not
require a global labeling of the zero set.

Lemma 6.3 (Nearest feet and multiplicity). On ΣS , for S sufficiently large, let l(z) be the shortest
Euclidean distance to a distinct sheet in the local good chart. This definition is independent of the
chart. Uniformly as s(z) → ∞,

                                    l(z) −→ ∞,          l(z) = o(s(z)).

Locally l is the minimum of the smooth distance functions to the one or two adjacent sheets. On an
active branch lb , with nearest foot z ′ = πb (z), one has

                                                                C
                                ΔΣ lb ≤ |H(z)| + |H(z ′ )| +        |∇Σ lb |2 .                     (6.8)
                                                               s(z)

Every measurable choice of an active foot has inverse multiplicity at most two. The branch area
Jacobian is 1 + oS (1), and

                 s(z ′ )/s(z) = 1 + oS (1),    |z ′ − y|/|z − y| = 1 + oS (1)     (y ∈ Z).

Proof. Defining the adjacent foot. All K ≥ 2 graphs have height o(s); their separation diverges,
so the nearest distinct-sheet distance has the claimed size. Orientations alternate because the
solution changes sign on crossing each regular zero graph. Equivalently, l is the nearest distance
to a zero whose oriented normal has scalar product less than −1/2 with ν(z). The minimum is
attained within o(s), and every competitor that could improve it lies in the same buffered chart. A
nonadjacent sheet cannot minimize: the chord to it crosses an intervening zero sheet at a strictly
smaller distance. This also proves that the definition agrees on overlapping charts.
    Projection to an adjacent sheet is smooth at these distances. Indeed its curvature is O(s−1 ), the
distance is o(s), and the slopes are small. In graph coordinates the squared-distance function has
positive definite Hessian near its minimizing foot; all possible minimizers lie in this region, since
their horizontal displacements are at most l = o(s). It thus has a unique foot.
    The branch Laplacian. Choose on the target graph the unit normal N pointing toward the
source. Then z = z ′ + lb N (z ′ ), and the distance Hessian at z has eigenvalues
                                                 κi
                                                        ≤ κi
                                              1 + lb κi
in the transported target tangent directions, and zero in the normal direction. Here 1 + lb κi > 0; its
operator norm is O(s−1 ). The inequality follows exactly by subtracting lb κ2i /(1 + lb κi ). Restricting


                                                   29
to the source surface removes its normal-normal Hessian component. Its absolute value is bounded
by
                                  C(                    C
                                     1 − (ν(z) · N )2 = |∇Σ lb |2 .
                                                     )
                                  s                     s
The restriction formula for the Laplacian adds −H(z)ν(z) · N , of size at most |H(z)|. Taking the
ambient trace and bounding the target trace by |H(z ′ )| proves (6.8).
    The Jacobian and the count of two. The exact projection area Jacobian is

                                              |ν(z) · N (z ′ )|
                              Jπb (z) =                             = 1 + oS (1).                    (6.9)
                                          | det(Id + lb AN (z ′ ))|

To check multiplicity globally, fix a target point z ′ . Write l(z) ≤ εS s(z) on ΣS , where εS → 0. The
Lipschitz property of s gives
                                                        εS
                                         |z − z ′ | ≤        s(z ′ ).
                                                      1 − εS
Choose S so this fraction is smaller than the fixed inner radius of a good chart. Every active source
projecting to z ′ then lies in that single chart centered at z ′ . The fixed normal line through z ′ meets
each small-slope graph at most once. Any intervening sheet would meet the source-target segment
and contradict minimality. Only the immediately adjacent sheet on either side can therefore supply
a source, giving at most two preimages. This reasoning also covers ties between active branches. A
countable atlas and a measurable selection among the finite local branches permit the area formula
with this multiplicity. Finally s and distance to a fixed y ∈ Z are 1-Lipschitz and s(z) ≤ |z − y|; the
two ratio estimates follow from l = o(s).

   Let ψ be nonnegative, increasing and concave on the range of l, and set w = min{T0 , ψ(l)}. If

                               −ψ ′′ (l)/ψ ′ (l) ≥ C/s,           0 ≤ ψ ′ (l) ≤ L,

Lemma 6.3 gives                               (                         )
                                ΔΣ w ≤ L |H(z)| + |H(π(z))|                 on ΣS                  (6.10)
in the sense of measures, where any measurable active foot may be chosen almost everywhere.
To justify the weak statement, each local distance branch is smooth with locally bounded second
derivatives. Their finite minimum, including the constant cap, is locally semiconcave. Its singular
Hessian part is nonpositive, and its absolutely continuous Hessian agrees almost everywhere with
that of an active branch. On the capped part the constant branch has zero Laplacian√and the
right-hand side is nonnegative. The chain rule therefore gives (6.10). In particular ψ(l) = l works
with L = 1 for large S, since l = o(s).

6.4    A Green test and a first bound on the gap
The concave transformation in (6.10) absorbs the gradient error in the distance Laplacian. The next
test integrates this inequality against an inward Green kernel. Its cutoff errors must be independent
of the cap T0 , except for a geometric tail whose coefficient can be made small before the outer radius
is increased.

Proof. The cap can affect the radial geometric error, but it must not multiply the errors near the fixed
inner boundary or the bad set. We keep those three terms separate. Choose a smooth radial α, zero
on r ≤ R0 , one on r ≥ 2R0 , with derivatives bounded by CR0−j . Test (6.10) against the nonnegative
compactly supported αχϕR . Its support lies in the regular open surface, so distributional integration
by parts has no singular-set boundary. For a radial function f , the convention H = divΣ ν gives

                     ΔΣ f = f ′′ + (m − 1)f ′ /r + (f ′ /r − f ′′ )(er · ν)2 − Hf ′ (er · ν).

absolute value by                      ∫

On a dyadic shell of radius a, (6.6) bounds this by a quantity tending to zero as a → ∞, uniformly

uniform small bound on all later shells. This is the required Cesaro estimate.
    On the support of F (r/R), α = 1 for large R. The main term on the left is therefore exactly
that in (6.11). Bound the nonnegative right-hand test by r2−m ; all remaining terms have just been
estimated.

attains the logarithmic cap.

                                                      33
6.6   The local regularity consequence
The classification has now made every finite threshold subcritical. The following consequence
therefore applies a local density bound at one large radius; it does not require a global density
bound for the particular entire solution to which it is applied.

Corollary 6.6 (Uniform regularity from any finite density bound). Fix 4 ≤ n ≤ 7 and 0 < Kf < ∞.
There are c > 0, C < ∞, r0 < ∞, depending only on n, Kf , such that every entire stable parameter-
one solution with Mr (z) ≤ Kf , r ≥ r0 , satisfies
                                         ⏐ ∇v ⏐
                                         ⏐      ⏐
                                −1
                     |∇v| ≥ C            ⏐ |∇v| ⏐ ≤ C/r      on Bcr (z) ∩ {|v| ≤ 0.9}.
                                     ,   ⏐∇     ⏐


For each prescribed slope tolerance and each fixed ι > 0, if v(z) = 0, a sufficiently thin slab
confinement of the zero set in this ball about a hyperplane through z gives, on a smaller cylinder,
ordered graphs with that slope tolerance and

                       ∥∇2 fi ∥∞ + r1/2 [∇2 fi ]C 1/2 ≤ C ′ /r,    |Hi | ≤ Cι′ r−2+ι .

The constants and interior fractions depend only on the stated parameters. On each fixed window
about an inner zero, the solution converges smoothly to its oriented planar profile as r → ∞,
uniformly among these solutions.

Proof. Theorem 1.3 makes every finite Kf subcritical. Apply [10, Theorem 3.2] with its tolerance
δ(Kf , n) and r−1 ≤ δ; its allowed density is Kf + δ, so the stated bound suffices. Its sheeting
assumptions and [10, Theorem 2.14, Theorem 4.8, and Lemmas 4.9–4.10] give the remaining
assertions, as specified above. Constants ±1 have an empty core band and cause no exception.


Part III
Geometry and stability without a density
bound
7     Penalized reach and isolated interfaces
We now work in the only case left by finite-density rigidity: an entire stable solution v : ℝ7 → (−1, 1)
with D > 0 and unbounded energy density. Thus the interface dimension is m = 6. Our aim is to
attach a length to each regular zero that controls both bending of its own sheet and approach to
another sheet. We will prove a differential inequality for this length, including a precise rule for
integrating the contribution of a second contact point.
    Recall that Σ = {v = 0} and, on its regular part, ν = ∇U/|∇U |, A = dν|T Σ , a = |A|, and
H = tr A. For the opposite orientation, or a separately specified unit normal N , we write AN and
HN . The normal map is
                                         (z, t) |−→ z + tν(z).
It can lose local invertibility when Id + tA becomes singular; even before that, normals from different
parts of Σ can meet. The reach below records both obstructions through chords between zeros.
    There is still useful local regularity in this setting. Corollary 6.6 applies whenever one large ball
has a fixed finite density bound; it does not require a density bound on the whole solution. We first

                                                     34
find such a ball at almost the reach scale. Its isolated graph supports two parity projections that
improve mean-curvature derivatives. A separate tangent-ball argument controls the signed mean
curvature at a possibly distant contact. These are the two local inputs to the reach inequality.
    Throughout, put
                                     K(R) = 1 + sup MR (x).
                                                     x∈ℝ7

Monotonicity and the uniform energy-integrand bound give 1 ≤ K(R) ≤ 1 + CR and K(R) → ∞.
All constants are uniform in the solution and in translating centers. When dependence on a fixed
penalty matters, it is indicated by CΓ . Dependence on K will always be displayed explicitly.

7.1    Chords, the penalty, and ordinary tubes
For distinct zeros z, y, with z regular, set ℓ = |y − z|, e = (y − z)/ℓ, and s = |ν(z) · e|. If s > 0, then
ℓ/s is twice the radius of the normal tangent ball at z passing through y. As y tends to z along a
principal direction, the same quotient tends to twice the reciprocal absolute principal curvature
when that curvature is nonzero. Thus taking an infimum over chords measures both approach and
bending.
    Figure 1 illustrates this quotient before we modify its angular factor.




                                           center    ℓ/(2s)
                                                               y∈Σ

                                             ν(z)
                                                         ℓe = y − z
                                                                      Tz Σ
                                                z

Figure 1: The normal-centered circle through two zeros, shown on the side where ν(z) · e = s > 0.
Its radius is ℓ/(2s), so the unmodified quotient ℓ/s is twice that radius. The blue arc shows only
the local sheet at z; the picture asserts neither tangency at y nor that the circle bounds a zero-free
ball. The penalized reach replaces 1/s by Γ(s) below.

    We slightly modify this quotient when the chord is almost normal. The angular factor Γ(s)
will equal 1/s away from s = 1, stay uniformly close to 1/s, and be constant near 1. Its constant
portion makes the second contact normal to the chord; this is what will permit the signed transfer in
Proposition 7.7. The transition must also have small weighted second derivative, so its differentiation
costs only an arbitrarily small error.
    Here is a penalty with these properties. Fix an accuracy ε1 > 0, choose 0 < δ1 < 1/10, then
L > 1, and put δ2 = δ1 e−L . Let χ : ℝ → [0, 1] be smooth, nonincreasing, equal to 1 on (−∞, 0], and
equal to 0 on [1, ∞). For s ≥ 1 − δ1 define
                                          ∫ s
                                   1                   log(δ1 /(1 − u))
                                                     (                  )
                                                  −2
                         Γ(s) =         −       u χ                       du,
                                 1 − δ1    1−δ1                L

and set Γ(s) = s−1 below 1 − δ1 . The integrand is understood as zero near u = 1. This gives a

smooth function on (0, 1], constant, with value G1 , for s ≥ 1 − δ2 . Direct differentiation gives

                           s−1 ≤ Γ(s) ≤ (1 + Cδ1 )s−1 ,            1 ≤ G1 ≤ Γ(s),

   On Σreg define
                                                                        y−z
                  d(z) =     inf     ℓΓ(s),        ℓ = |y − z|,    e=       ,   s = |ν(z) · e|.               (7.2)

A competitor with s = 0 has value +∞. An active contact is a competitor attaining this infimum,
or a limiting diagonal competitor attaining it as the second point approaches the base. The proof of
Proposition 7.7 will show that one of these alternatives always realizes the infimum.

Lemma 7.1 (Elementary chord bounds). The function d is finite and positive on Σreg . At every
such point, writing λi for the eigenvalues of A,

                   d max |λi | ≤ 2,           |(y − z) · ν(z)| ≤ CΓ |y − z|2 /d(z)   (y ∈ Σ).                 (7.3)
                       i

For each J < ∞ there is a uniform δ(J) > 0 such that v(z) = 0 and D(z) < δ(J) imply d(z) > J.
Moreover, at every regular base there are zero-free open balls tangent at that base on both normal
sides, of radius cΓ d(z).

Proof. Local smooth graph representation bounds the quotients |ν(z) · (y − z)|/|y − z|2 for nearby
zeros; competitors outside a fixed small ball have a positive lower bound because Γ ≥ 1. This proves
positivity. If every zero lay in the tangent plane at z, phase decay (5.14) would bound the energy by
Ce−c dist(x,Tz Σ) , giving finite density. Thus a finite competitor exists. A signed unit geodesic tangent
to a principal direction gives ℓ/s → 2/|λi | whenever λi ̸= 0, proving the first inequality. The second
uses the upper penalty bound: d ≤ ℓΓ(s) ≤ CΓ ℓ/s.
    If D(zj ) → 0, the translated, rotated fields U converge smoothly on every fixed ball to xn , by
(5.9) and the equation. In B4J all zeros are therefore one graph whose curvature and slope tend to
zero. A competitor of value at most J has ℓ ≤ J; on this graph s ≤ o(1)ℓ, whereas ℓΓ(s) ≥ ℓ/s. This
is impossible, also for competitors converging to the diagonal. The contradiction formulation proves
uniformity of the threshold. Finally, the second inequality in (7.3) excludes zeros from each ball
BcΓ d (z±cΓ dν) when cΓ is sufficiently small: a point in either ball has |ν ·(y−z)| > |y−z|2 /(2cΓ d).

Remark 7.2 (Jointly injective ordinary tubes). The elementary chord inequality also gives the
ordinary tube used in the final packing argument. If d(z) > L, choose 0 < c < min{1/(2CΓ ), 1/2}.
For 0 < |t| < cL and every other zero y,

             |z + tν(z) − y|2 − t2 = |y − z|2 − 2tν(z) · (y − z) ≥ (1 − 2cCΓ )|y − z|2 > 0.

Thus z is the unique nearest zero to z + tν(z). Normal tubes over all bases with d > L are
therefore jointly injective in this range, on both sides. The curvature bound max |λi | ≤ 2/d implies
det(Id + tA) ≥ (1 − 2c)m > 0. Consequently any such bases in B2R have area at most CRm+1 /L
when L ≤ R, by integrating the positive Jacobian over both normal sides and comparing the
resulting tube volume to |B3R |.


                                                         36
7.2   A bounded density window with delayed monotonicity
A long reach confines nearby zeros to a thin slab. We use that confinement to limit the increase
of energy density between successive radii. The resulting estimate involves the density at a larger
radius, so its discrete iteration must retain that delay.

Lemma 7.3 (A fixed density bound near the reach scale). There are fixed C, c > 0 and a fixed
density bound K0 < ∞ such that, if d = d(z) is sufficiently large, then
                                         √
                          h = d exp{−C log(K(d) + 1)},            Mh (z) ≤ K0 .                     (7.4)

The constant C can be increased without invalidating the conclusion. In particular h ≥ d1−o(1) ,
uniformly as d → ∞.

Proof. Slab confinement and one-step density control. Translate z to 0 and take ν(z) = en . By
(7.3), the zeros in B3R lie in |xn | ≤ CR2 /d. With 𝕊 = e(v)Id − ∇v ⊗ ∇v, the stress tensor identity
div 𝕊 = 0, tested against xn en ζ 2 , gives
                                       ∫           ∫
                                           2
                                         ζ 𝕊nn = −2 xn ζ 𝕊en · ∇ζ.

                        √
The identities ∇v = qp/ 2 and e(v) = q 2 (1 + |p|2 )/4 show 𝕊nn ≥ cq 2 (1 − p2n ) and |𝕊en |2 ≤ Cq 2 𝕊nn .
Young’s inequality, with a cutoff equal to one on BR and supported on B2R , consequently proves
                               ∫                     ∫
                                     2      2
                                    q (1 − pn ) ≤ C       q 2 (xn /R)2 .

                                             √
On the part of B2R with |xn | > CR2 /d + R, every zero either lies outside B3R or has the confined
                                       √                                                               √
height, so its distance is at least c R. Phase decay controls the energy there by CRn e−c R .
Elsewhere (xn /R)2 ≤ C((R/d)2 + R−1 ).
   In the monotonicity formula between R/B and R, with a fixed B ≥ 8, the discrepancy is bounded
by Cq 2 (1 − p2n ), and the squared radial derivative on these annuli by CB q 2 (1 − p2n + (xn /R)2 ). The
preceding estimate, at a fixed multiple of R, therefore gives

              0 ≤ MR (z) − MR/B (z) ≤ C (R/d)2 + R−1 MBR (z)
                                             (                )
                                                                          (10 ≤ R ≤ cd).            (7.5)

The exponentially small remainder was absorbed using MR (z) ≥ c for R ≥ 1. This uniform lower
bound follows from v(z) = 0, the uniform first derivative bound, and monotonicity.
    Iterating with the larger-radius term. We now retain the delay in (7.5); replacing MBR by MR
would give an unjustified recursion. Set Rj = dB −j , kj = MRj (z), and bj = C(B −2j + Rj−1 ). Beyond
a fixed starting index,
                                         kj − kj+1 ≤ bj kj−1 .
                                                                                  √
Choose Rfix and that starting index so bj < 1/8 while Rj ≥ Rfix . Put J = ⌈C0 log(K(d) + 1)⌉. For
sufficiently large d, R2J+10 ≥ d1/2 , because K(d) ≤ 1 + Cd. Suppose a halving kj+1 < kj /2 occurs at
some j ≥ J − 2 before the fixed-scale endpoint. Then kj−1 > kj /(2bj ) > 4kj , so a halving also occurs
at the preceding index. Induction propagates this backwards through all indices in [⌈J/2⌉, J − 3].
On this interval bi ≤ Ce−cJ : the B −2i term has this bound, and Ri−1 ≤ d−1/2 is smaller. Multiplying
the preceding lower growth factors over at least cJ indices gives K(d) ≥ c exp(c′ J 2 ), contradicting
the choice of large C0 . Here all kj before the endpoint are bounded below by the same positive
constant. Thus no such halving occurs.

                                                   37
   It follows that kj−1 ≤ 2kj for j ≥ J up to that endpoint, and hence kj+1 ≥ (1 − 2bj )kj . The
sum of bj on this range is uniformly bounded: the first terms form a geometric series, and the
Rj−1 terms form a reversed geometric series ending at a fixed radius. The product of (1 − 2bj )−1 is
therefore bounded. At the last index MRj has a uniform bound from the bounded energy integrand.
Consequently kJ ≤ K0 with fixed K0 . Taking C large enough in (7.4) makes h ≤ RJ ; monotonicity
completes the proof.

7.3    A bounded positive barrier for the normal operator
The profile error on either side of an isolated zero sheet vanishes on that sheet. To compare this error
with its forcing we need a positive bounded supersolution for the normal operator. The following
formula will also apply when the boundary of a later comparison region is shifted by a fixed amount.
                                                                  √
