7.4    Isolation and the two parity projections
The density window now gives one graph on the scale h. For reach calculus we need more than
its curvature bound: the first two derivatives of H must be smaller than the scale-invariant h−3
size. Two projections in the normal variable provide this gain. The first uses oddness of the leading
curvature forcing; the second uses the tangential improvement obtained from the first.
Lemma 7.5 (An isolated graph and mean-curvature derivatives). There are uniform c, c′ , C, γ > 0
such that, for sufficiently large d(z) and h in (7.4), the zero set in Bch (z) is a single graph over
Tz Σ, extending over the tangent disk of radius c′ h. On a smaller concentric portion,
                  |A| + h1/2 [A]C 1/2 ≤ Ch−1 ,      |∇Σ A| ≤ Ch−2 ,          |H| ≤ Ch−2 ,
                                                                                                     (7.7)
                                      |∇Σ H| + |∇2Σ H| ≤ Ch−3−γ .
The estimates hold uniformly on every inner patch having a fixed fractional margin from the original
chart boundary. Hölder norms may be computed in its graph coordinates.

                                                    38
Proof. One graph and preliminary bounds. The fixed bound of Lemma 7.3 is subcritical by The-
orem 1.3. Apply Corollary 6.6, including its sheeting conclusions, at radius h. Thus the density
used here is the fixed local bound K0 ; the contradiction hypothesis still allows unbounded density
on larger scales. The confinement thickness divided by this radius is O(h/d). Increasing the fixed
constant in (7.4) makes this ratio as small as required, without changing the density bound. The
resulting graphs have arbitrarily small fixed slope, the first estimate in (7.7), and

                                  |H| ≤ Cι h−2+ι    for every fixed ι > 0.                         (7.8)

An additional graph in an inner cylinder would cross the normal line at z at distance O(h), producing
a competitor of value O(h), contrary to d/h being sufficiently large. Thus just one sheet remains
in a smaller cylinder. On fixed windows centered on inner zeros the solution, in oriented normal
coordinates, converges uniformly and smoothly to g. The lower gradient bound from sheeting and
interior elliptic estimates also give unit-scale bounds on all fixed orders of graph derivatives. These
statements are uniform in the centers and solutions, by the stated uniformity of the subcritical ball
input and the usual sequential formulation of compactness.
    It remains to strengthen the mean-curvature derivatives and then recover the large-scale bound
on ∇Σ A. The two projections below serve different purposes: the first removes the leading odd
forcing from the equation at the zero sheet, and the second uses the resulting tangential gain
to improve the derivatives of H. We prove the derivative improvement; in particular we do not
differentiate (7.8) at its original scale. Fix an inner zero as origin. Let X(y) be its tangent-plane
graph and use Fermi coordinates X(y) + tν(y). Choose a small number ϵ > 0, to be fixed at the
end, and write Rh = hϵ . All arguments below take place in a fixed finite sequence of nested boxes
with side lengths fixed fractions of Rh , starting inside {|y|∞ , |t| < Rh }. The much larger h-scale
chart guarantees that these coordinates are injective there, have polynomially bounded unit-scale
derivatives of each fixed order, and contain no other zero. The distance to Σ is comparable to |t| on
the boxes. Consequently
                                 φ(y, t) = v(X(y) + tν(y)) − g(t) = o(1)
uniformly. To see uniformity also at unbounded t, first choose a large fixed depth and use phase
decay beyond it, then use the fixed window convergence at shallower depths. For |t| a fixed fraction
of Rh , φ and every needed fixed derivative are bounded by ChC e−cRh : apply phase decay and
unit-scale elliptic estimates to v ∓ 1, and differentiate the coordinate composition.
    We record the interpolation used below. If a tensor has uniformly bounded C N norm on unit
balls and C 1/2 seminorm at most Ch−3/2 , its derivatives of orders 1 ≤ k ≤ 30 are bounded by
Ch−5/4 , by taking N sufficiently large once and for all. Indeed the standard interpolation exponent
of the low seminorm can be taken arbitrarily close to one for any fixed k by increasing N ; subtracting
its value at the center removes the constant term. Applied to the coordinate components of A,
whose change-of-frame terms have the same or better bound, this gives

               |∂yk A| ≤ Ch−5/4     (1 ≤ k ≤ 30),        |∂yk H| ≤ Cϵ h−2+ϵ   (0 ≤ k ≤ 30).        (7.9)

For the second assertion use (7.8) with ι < ϵ/2 and interpolate its supremum norm with a sufficiently
high unit-scale derivative bound. All interpolation orders are finite and fixed before h tends to
infinity.
    In these coordinates the equation is exactly

             (−Δy − ∂t2 + Vg )φ = Kt g ′ + Kt φt + (gtij − δ ij )∂ij φ + bit ∂i φ − 3gφ2 − φ3 ,
                                                                                                  (7.10)
                               Vg = 3g 2 − 1,       Kt = tr[A(Id + tA)−1 ] .

                                                    39
Here gtij is the inverse tangential metric, and bit is the first-order coefficient in its Laplace operator.
Neither superscript nor subscript t denotes differentiation in this display. Matrix inversion in Id + tA,
the graph metric, and (7.9) give, with derivatives through order 20,

                   Kt = H − t|A|2 + t2 tr A3 + O(h−4+Cϵ ),          Kt = O(h−2+Cϵ ),
                                                                                                    (7.11)
                             gtij − δ ij = O(h−1+Cϵ ),     bit = O(h−1+Cϵ ).

Every occurrence of Cϵ in this proof has a bounded absolute multiplier independent of ϵ. For
example the tangent slope is O(h−1+ϵ ), and the remainder in the first line contains at least four
factors of A and at most a fixed power of |t|. The differentiated remainders obey the same bounds,
since each positive derivative of A is no larger than O(h−1 ).
    A first bound for the profile error. Use v0 from Lemma 7.4 on t ≥ 0 and its reflection on
t ≤ 0, separately on the two half-boxes. The reflected pair need not be differentiable at zero; no
full-line supersolution is asserted. In each half-box move the metric and drift terms to the left of
(7.10) and write the nonlinearity as (3gφ + φ2 )φ. Its coefficient tends uniformly to zero, so the
resulting operator applied to v0 is at least 1/2. Comparison is valid after division by this positive
supersolution, which makes the zeroth-order coefficient positive. For each lateral or distant normal
face, v0 e−c0 distance to face is also a supersolution when c0 is a sufficiently small fixed number. The
sum of these face barriers and Ch−2+Cϵ v0 dominates both signs of φ. The bottom boundary has
φ(y, 0) = 0. Shrinking the box makes all face contributions exponentially small. Interior elliptic
estimates across t = 0, using the smooth equation, then give

                                          ∥φ∥C 15 ≤ Cϵ h−2+Cϵ .                                     (7.12)

The coefficient norms needed for this bootstrap are uniform by (7.11); the nonlinearity is smooth
and has already been bounded in supremum norm.
    The inverse on the complement of the translation mode. The positive barrier controlled the
full profile error. The next estimate separates its translation mode from the remaining normal
modes, where a spectral gap is available. Let Lt = −∂t2 + Vg . It has kernel spanned by g ′ and
a fixed spectral gap λ > 0 on its L2 (ℝ) orthogonal complement. One elementary justification
is the ground-state factorization of its quadratic form, simplicity of the positive zero mode, and
compactness on bounded intervals together with Vg → 2 at infinity; a Rayleigh sequence tending to
zero could neither escape to infinity nor stay orthogonal to the kernel. Let P⊥ be this projection.
For
                               (−Δy + Lt )w = P⊥ F,       P⊥ w = w,
the regularized L2t norm of each y-derivative of w is a subsolution for −Δy + λ, with source the
corresponding norm of F . This follows by differentiating the squared norm, using ⟨Lt w, w⟩ ≥ λ∥w∥22
and Cauchy–Schwarz, and then letting the regularization vanish. Comparison with the constant
source bound plus decaying face barriers controls these norms on each smaller y-box, with an
exponentially small multiple of their boundary bound. Commuting two additional y-derivatives and
using the equation gives the same bound in Ht2 , since ∥w∥H 2 ≤ C(∥Lt w∥2 + ∥w∥2 ). Thus source
derivatives through order j + 2 control the value at t = 0 and its y-derivatives through order j.
This assertion applies separately to even and odd functions of t, because both the operator and
P⊥ preserve parity. It applies also to a range of strictly positive derivative orders: no estimate of
the undifferentiated source is needed to estimate a differentiated equation. Polynomial boundary
bounds suffice in every use here.
    The whole-line equation and the first parity projection. Choose an even cutoff χh (t), equal to
one on an inner normal interval and supported in a slightly larger interval inside the box, with

                                                    40
derivatives supported where |t| is comparable to Rh . Put Φ = χh φ. If Eraw denotes the right side of
(7.10) minus Hg ′ , the exact equation on the whole normal line is

          (−Δy + Lt )Φ = Hg ′ + Efull ,      Efull = χh Eraw + (χh − 1)Hg ′ − 2χ′h φt − χ′′h φ.

The kernel tail (χh − 1)Hg ′ and the two commutators, together with all their needed y-derivatives,
are O(h−N ) for every fixed N . For the tail this follows from the preliminary derivatives of H and the
exponential decay of g ′ ; for the commutators it follows from the normal-edge estimates. Restoring
the cutoff leading term −χh t|A|2 g ′ to the whole-line odd function −t|A|2 g ′ costs another error of
the same order. We henceforth write φ for Φ, and E for Efull . Write

               φ = u0 (y)g ′ (t) + φ⊥ (y, t),    (−Δy + Lt )φ = H(y)g ′ (t) + E(y, t).
                                    ∫
We normalize kernel coefficients by (g ′ )2 . From (7.11) and (7.12), in L2t with y-derivatives through
order 11,
                      E = −t|A|2 g ′ + O(h−3+Cϵ ),        E even = O(h−3+Cϵ ).                    (7.13)
Here t2 tr A3 g ′ has size h−3+Cϵ , every metric error is a coefficient of size h−1+Cϵ times a derivative

  ⊥

                                           −Δy u0 = H + E0                                         (7.14)

                                ∥∂yk φ∥L2t ≤ Ch−2−1/4+Cϵ ,    1 ≤ k ≤ 8.                          (7.15)

For these L2t bounds no loss of two derivative orders is required; the already controlled derivatives
of u0 cover the kernel term.
    The first projection has now bounded the kernel coefficient through nine tangential derivatives
and H through seven. The full orthogonal equation has also supplied the gain in (7.15) without
losing derivative orders. We use that gain in a second even projection; no eighth derivative of H is
required, because P⊥ annihilates Hg ′ .
    The second parity projection. Return to (7.10). Positive derivatives through order 6 of the even
part of E now obey
                            ∥∂yk E even ∥L2t ≤ Ch−3−1/4+Cϵ ,   1 ≤ k ≤ 6.                      (7.16)

Indeed the odd term −t|A|2 g ′ still contributes nothing; a positive derivative of tr A3 contains a factor
bounded by h−5/4 ; every horizontal metric or drift error contains at least one positive y-derivative
of φ, so (7.15) applies up to order 8. If derivatives instead fall on its coefficient, that coefficient
remains O(h−1+Cϵ ) and the same estimate applies to the positive derivative already present on
φ. The normal drift and nonlinear terms remain O(h−4+Cϵ ) in these norms. This accounts for all
terms, including products of derivatives and the superpolynomially small cutoff errors.
    Apply the even orthogonal inverse once more. Its value at zero and the zero condition give
|∂y u0 | ≤ Ch−3−1/4+Cϵ for 1 ≤ k ≤ 4. Equation (7.14) gives the same estimate for ∂y H and ∂y2 H.
  k

Choose ϵ so small that every fixed exponent loss Cϵ above is less than 1/8; decreasing the resulting


                                                   41
gain if necessary gives γ > 0. Covariant derivatives have the asserted bounds as well: the coordinate
connection is bounded and its extra terms multiply already controlled first derivatives of H.
    Recovering the large-scale curvature derivative. The improved mean-curvature derivatives are
now in hand. Rescale the original graph disk by h−1 . Its existing C 2,1/2 bound is uniform, and the
rescaled mean curvature hH has uniformly bounded C 1,1 norm, since its first and second derivatives
scale by h2 and h3 , respectively. Differentiate the prescribed mean-curvature graph equation once
and apply interior Schauder estimates. The rescaled graph has a uniform third derivative bound,
giving |∇Σ A| ≤ Ch−2 in original variables. All boxes used above have a fixed total fractional
shrinkage and all derivative orders were fixed in advance. Repeating at each inner center proves the
uniform inner-window assertion.

7.5    One-sided trace improvement from a tangent phase ball
The isolated chart controls the base point of a reach contact. Its second point need not lie in that
chart, so it requires an estimate whose sole geometric input is a zero-free tangent ball. The sign of
the resulting trace bound matters: only positive mean curvature at the receiving point will enter
the upper reach inequality.

Lemma 7.6 (One-sided improvement at a tangent phase ball). There are uniform r0 , C, γ > 0 with
the following property. If an open ball of radius r ≥ r0 is disjoint from Σ and its boundary contains
x ∈ Σ, and N is its inward normal at x, then

                 x ∈ Σreg ,       D(x) ≤ C/r,       a(x) ≤ C/r,        HN (x) ≤ Cr−1−γ .            (7.17)

After changing the sign of v so that it is positive inside the ball, along the inward radius one also has

                              U (x + sN ) ≥ s − C(1 + s)/r       (0 ≤ s ≤ log r).                   (7.18)

Only the last curvature estimate is one-sided; no absolute-value improvement for HN is asserted.

Proof. Comparison along the inward radius. Let s denote inward depth in the annulus 0 < s < Q =
√
  r. The nonnegative function
                                        ∫ s            ∫ ∞
                                   ′
                           w(s) = g (s)       ′
                                            (g (t)) −2
                                                           (g ′ (u))2 du dt
                                              0              t

has w(0) = 0, (−∂s2 +Vg )w = g ′ , and |w|+|w′ | ≤ C(1+s)g ′ . Thus v∗ = g −C0 w/r, with a sufficiently
large fixed C0 , is positive on the annulus and satisfies Δv∗ + v∗ − v∗3 > 0. Indeed the linear correction
contributes C0 g ′ /r, the inward radial drift is −(n − 1)v∗′ /(r − s), and the quadratic correction is
bounded by Cr−2 (1 + s)2 (g ′ )2 = o(r−1 g ′ ) uniformly on [0, Q]. Multiplication by 1 − e−cQ , with a
sufficiently small fixed c > 0, keeps a strict subsolution and makes its value less than v on the inner
boundary, by phase decay. On the outer boundary it vanishes. Sliding its amplitude from a small
positive number to one proves comparison. To justify the start, v > 0 in the compact annulus and
the Hopf boundary estimate applies at any outer zero; these and compactness bound the quotient
from below. At a first contact a strict subsolution cannot touch in the interior, and the Hopf
comparison excludes an outer boundary obstruction. Smaller amplitudes remain strict subsolutions:
writing ℱ(w) = Δw + w − w3 , one has ℱ(av∗ ) = aℱ(v∗ ) + (a − a3 )v∗3 > 0 for 0 < a ≤ 1.
    Inverting
          √    g on 0 ≤ s ≤ log r proves (7.18): the correction is bounded by C(1 + s)g ′ (s)/r, and the
extra e −c  r term is smaller after division by g ′ (s) on that range. Differentiation at the contact point
gives ∂N v(x) ≥ g ′ (0) − C/r > 0. Thus x is regular, N = ν after the sign choice, and D(x) ≤ C/r.


                                                    42
The tangent ball gives the principal-curvature lower bound λi (AN ) ≥ −1/r. The zero-level identity
HN = ∂N D/(2|p|2 ) and (5.9) give |HN | ≤ CD ≤ C/r. Summing the principal curvatures then
bounds each one from above by C/r, proving the full curvature estimate in (7.17).
    Separating the even source from the boundary contribution. The comparison has established
regularity and the r−1 bounds. To improve only the upper trace, place x = 0, N = en , and use the
ambient vertical coordinate s. Work in BP with P = α log r, where α > 0 is fixed sufficiently small
at the end. The differentiated diffuse estimates give, for every needed fixed order k,

             ∥D∥C k (BP ) ≤ Ck r−1+Cα ,     ∥T ∥C k (BP ) + ∥U − s∥C k (BP ) ≤ Ck r−1/2+Cα .

These follow by applying unit-ball derivative estimates and the exponential comparison for D
along chains from 0; integration of T gives the estimate on U − s. The multipliers of α are fixed
independently of small α. The identity ∇p T = −T 2 − ∇2 D/2 further gives ∂s T = O(r−1+Cα ): the
replacement of p by en has error |p − en ||∇T | = O(r−1+Cα ).
    Set F = G(s)D. Conjugating the defect equation and replacing vp by g(s)en and q|p|2 by G(s)
yields
               (−Δ + 2 − G(s))F = 2G(s)|T (x′ , 0)|2 + ℰ,     ∥ℰ∥∞ ≤ Cr−3/2+Cα .           (7.19)
For completeness, each replacement in the drift or potential multiplies a coefficient of size r−1/2+Cα
by D or ∇D of size r−1+Cα . Replacing |T (x′ , s)|2 by |T (x′ , 0)|2 has the same order, since |T | =
O(r−1/2+Cα ), ∂s T = O(r−1+Cα ), and |s| ≤ P . Logarithmic factors are absorbed by increasing the
fixed multiplier of α.
     Solve the Dirichlet problem on BP by linearity. The solution with source 2G|T (x′ , 0)|2 and zero
boundary is even in s and nonnegative. The zero-boundary solution for ℰ has interior C 1 norm at
most Cr−3/2+Cα , since the potential 2 − G is at least one. The remaining homogeneous solution is
u+ +u− , both nonnegative, with boundary values F on the upper and lower hemispheres, respectively,
and zero on the other one. Their harmless equatorial boundary discontinuity is interpreted by
bounded Dirichlet solutions; it does not affect interior estimates. Reflection and the maximum
principle in the upper half-ball give ∂s u− (0) ≤ 0; local Harnack and derivative estimates give
|∂s u+ (0)| ≤ Cu+ (0).
     At x∗ = (0, P/2), positivity of the source solution implies

                u+ (x∗ ) ≤ F (x∗ ) + Cr−3/2+Cα ≤ C(1 + P )r−1 G(P/2) + Cr−3/2+Cα .

For the last inequality integrate D ≤ 2(1 − ∂s U ) along [0, P/2 + 1], use (7.18), and apply unit-scale
Harnack to D to pass from the integral to its value at P/2. This use is valid for α < 1, so the
interval lies in the range of (7.18).
    Suppressing the upper boundary contribution. Only u+ can contribute a positive leading normal
derivative at the origin. Its value at x∗ is small by the inward∫ comparison; we now transfer that
gain to the origin with explicit Poisson bounds. Write A∂ = ∂BP ∩{s>0} F dS. Comparison with
potentials one and two gives
                                                             √     √
                 u+ (0) ≤ CP C e−P A∂ ,      u+ (x∗ ) ≥ cζ e−( 2+ζ) 5P/2 A∂    (ζ > 0).           (7.20)

For the first inequality, the Poisson density at the center for −Δ + 1 is constant on the sphere.
The spherical average of ex·θ shows that its size is at most CP C e−P . The solution for potential
2 − G ≥ 1 is smaller. For the second, the solution for potential 2 − G ≤ 2 is larger than  √ that for
−Δ + 2. Its Dirichlet Poisson density at any upper-hemisphere point ξ is at least cζ e  −(  2+ζ)|x∗ −ξ| .

Here are quantitative details of this last bound. Start the Dirichlet Green function a fixed distance
from x∗ , with a fixed positive lower bound obtained from a fixed-radius ball. Along the segment

                                                                                 √          √
towards ξ insert a cylinder of fixed large cross-sectional radius, chosen so that 2 + λ1 < 2 + ζ.
Its first transverse Dirichlet mode times the longitudinal hyperbolic-sine solution propagates the

BP except for fixed initial and final portions: x∗ is at distance P/2 from the boundary and the

Since 10/2 − 1 − 2/2 < 0, choose ζ so the first exponent is negative, then choose α > 0 so

lower-hemisphere solution has nonpositive derivative there, and the error solution is smaller still.
Thus Fs (0) ≤ Cr−1−γ . Since G(0) = 1, G′ (0) = 0, and |p(0)|2 = 1 − O(r−1 ), the zero-level identity
for HN proves (7.17).

7.6   The weak reach inequality and transfer of positive charge
We now combine the base estimates with the tangent-ball bound. At an active contact on the plateau
of the penalty, the second point will be called the receiving foot. The proposition distinguishes
these contact types because only plateau contacts retain a mean-curvature term at that foot. It
also specifies the direction and multiplicity of the area change needed when that positive term is
integrated.
Proposition 7.7 (Weak reach inequality and positive-foot transfer). Given η0 > 0, the penalty
parameters can be chosen, in the order specified above, so that the following holds for all sufficiently
large d. The function d is locally semiconcave on Σreg , and the singular part of its distributional
surface Laplacian is nonpositive. Almost everywhere,
                                                       √
                                |∇Σ d| ≤ CΓ exp{CΓ log(K(d) + 1)}.                                      (7.21)

At an active non-plateau or diagonal contact,
                                                           |∇Σ d|2
                   ΔΣ d ≤ (1 + η0 + CΓ d−γ )a2 d + B               ,        B ≤ 1 + CΓ d−γ .            (7.22)
                                                             d
If ad is sufficiently small, the same bound can be used with B ≤ η0 + CΓ ad + CΓ d−γ , and then
|∇Σ d| ≥ cΓ > 0. At an active plateau contact, meaning s ≥ 1 − δ2 , one has
                                (                      )        |∇Σ d|2
                     ΔΣ d ≤ G1 |H(z)| + HNy (y)+ + η0                   ,       Ny = −e.                (7.23)

For any measurable choice of such contacts, the positive foot charge can be transferred to y with
multiplicity at most one and area factor
            | det(Id + ℓANy (y))|

More precisely, Jy→z ≤ 1 + Cδ2 + Ca(y)ℓ whenever a(y)ℓ is bounded by a sufficiently small fixed
constant. The constants and the large-reach threshold are fixed before any later defect threshold,
collar scale, or exhaustion radius is chosen.

                                                      44
Proof. Semiconcavity, including diagonal competitors. We first justify the weak interpretation,
independently of the differentiations to follow. On a compact regular coordinate patch there are
uniform bounds 0 < c ≤ d ≤ C: local graph curvature gives the lower bound, and one fixed
off-diagonal positive-angle competitor gives the upper bound after shrinking the patch. Relevant
competitors of value at most 2C have bounded length. Those of length at least ε > 0 have angle
s ≥ ε/(2C), by Γ ≥ 1/s. With the second foot fixed, these are smooth branches in the base variable,
uniformly bounded in C 2 ; no regularity of the second foot is needed here.
    For shorter chords the second point is on the same regular local graph. In coordinates X(u)
write it as X(u + tω), with |ω| = 1 and t small. Such chords use Γ = 1/s. The reciprocal branch is

                                     |ν(u) · [X(u + tω) − X(u)]|
                                                                 .
                                        |X(u + tω) − X(u)|2

Both numerator before absolute value and denominator vanish to second order in t, since DX is
tangent. Cancelling t2 by the integral Taylor formula gives a C 2 function through t = 0, uniformly
in the compact direction parameter. On relevant branches its absolute value is bounded below
by 1/(2C), so a smooth sign can be chosen and taking its reciprocal preserves the uniform C 2
bound. The diagonal limits are legitimate upper branches for d, since they are limits of competitors.
Compactness of the remaining parameter sets shows that every minimizing sequence has either an
off-diagonal or a diagonal realizing parameter and that d is the infimum of a family with locally
uniform upper Hessian bound. Subtracting the corresponding fixed quadratic function makes each
branch concave and hence also their infimum concave. This proves local semiconcavity.
    At almost every point there is an Alexandrov second-order expansion. At such a point any
smooth active upper branch has the same first derivative and an upper bound for the Hessian of d.
Its trace therefore bounds the absolutely continuous Laplacian. The singular part is a nonpositive
measure by semiconcavity, in coordinates and therefore for the surface Laplacian as well. It suffices
to compute the following upper touching branches.
    The weak interpretation is now established. We next compute smooth upper touching branches,
treating the unmodified penalty, diagonal limits, and the transition to the plateau in that order.
The final step will transfer the positive plateau contribution to its receiving foot.
    A tangent phase ball at every second foot. For an off-diagonal contact choose N = ±ν(z) so
N · e = s > 0. The open region

                                {z + ℓ′ e′ : N · e′ > 0, ℓ′ Γ(N · e′ ) < d}

contains no zeros. After translation, rotation, and division by d, its boundary is a fixed smooth
embedded compact radial surface. Near the origin it agrees with the sphere |x|2 = xn , because
there Γ(s) = 1/s. On the remainder it is the smooth positive radial graph 1/Γ. It has an inward
rolling ball of a fixed positive radius cΓ : local C 2 graph bounds supply the balls near each point,
and compactness and embeddedness allow a uniform decrease so those balls avoid the rest of the
boundary. Hence at the second foot y there is a zero-free tangent ball of radius cΓ d. Lemma 7.6
shows that the foot is regular, a(y) + D(y) ≤ CΓ /d, and its inward trace satisfies HNy (y) ≤ CΓ d−1−γ .
    Off-diagonal contacts with the unmodified penalty. First suppose s ≤ 1 − δ1 , so Γ = 1/s. Write

For clarity the leading coefficient is bounded by one up to O(δ1 ) because −sΓ′ /Γ ≤ 1 + Cδ1 ; the Γ′′
square contributes Cε1 a2 d and Cε1 |b|4 /d, and the rotation gives the remaining two terms. Young’s
inequality bounds Ca|b|2 by any fixed small multiple of a2 d plus a fixed multiple of |b|4 /d. If
ad is small, the first derivative formula bounds |∇d| below by a fixed multiple of |b|. If it is not
small, |b|4 /d is at most Cδ12 a2 d. Choosing ε1 and then δ1 small enough for the prescribed tolerance
therefore bounds all these terms by (1 + η0 )a2 d + η0 |∇d|2 /d, with room to decrease the initial
tolerance for the subsequent errors. In the transition interval before the plateau, |b| has a fixed
positive lower bound depending on δ2 . The trace terms in (7.27) are O(d−1−γ ) from above by the
base and second-foot estimates. They are absorbed exactly as in (7.25), proving (7.22), including its
                                                                 √
small-ad statement. The gradient formula, ℓ ≤ d, and da ≤ 2 m give a fixed bound in this range,
hence (7.21).
     On the plateau Γ′ = Γ′′ = 0, so ∇d = −G1 b, Z = G1 e, Ny = −e. The only algebraic remainder is
C|b|4 /d, bounded by Cδ2 |∇d|2 /d. The traces are G1 HNy + G1 sHN ≤ G1 (HNy ,+ + |HN |). Decreasing
δ2 if necessary proves (7.23). At the endpoints of the ranges the jets match, since the constructed
penalty is smooth; either computation thus has the same limiting validity.
    The differential inequalities have now been proved for every active contact type. It remains to
justify their integrated use: a positive plateau contribution at a receiving foot must be counted with
its inverse area factor, and without an additional multiplicity.
     The inverse Jacobian and positive receiving multiplicity. We prove the transfer statement in the
direction from receiving feet to bases; this direction determines the Jacobian below. For a plateau
pair, z = y + ℓNy and |Ny · ν(z)| = s ≥ 1 − δ2 . Fix a local regular sheet through z. The equation
that y + tNy lies on this sheet has nonzero t derivative, so the implicit function theorem supplies a
smooth inverse parametrization z(y) = y + ℓ(y)Ny near this pair. Projecting its derivative onto Ty Σ
removes ( dℓ)Ny and leaves Id + ℓANy . Orthogonal projection from Tz Σ to Ty Σ has area factor s. It
follows that the area Jacobian of the inverse map is exactly the quotient in (7.24). The second-foot
bound a(y) ≤ CΓ /d(z) therefore bounds this quotient uniformly. Expanding the determinant when
a(y)ℓ is small gives the more precise estimate stated. The reverse chord is normal at y, so it is a
competitor of value G1 ℓ there and proves d(y) ≤ d(z).
    At a foot with HNy > 0 the orientation Ny is unique. All bases sending positive charge to this
foot must lie on its same normal ray. Suppose two existed, at lengths 0 < ℓ1 < ℓ2 . For the farther
base z2 , the nearer base z1 is a competing zero with chord direction exactly equal to the direction

                                                      48
towards y and length ℓ2 − ℓ1 . It has the same angle penalty and strictly smaller objective than
the alleged active chord to y, a contradiction. Thus every positively charged foot has at most one
inverse base.
   There is no hidden diffeomorphism assumption here. Cover the regular pair relation by countably
many of the preceding inverse charts. Active parameters on compact coordinate pieces form a closed
compact-valued relation; a measurable selection can be obtained there and pieced together over
a countable exhaustion. Restrict the selected pairs to disjoint measurable pieces in those charts.
The area formula on each inverse chart gives exactly its Jacobian; its critical image has zero surface
measure. Summing the formula over the selected pieces adds no multiplicity for positive charges by
the ray argument. In explicit terms, for a nonnegative measurable integrand F (z, y) supported on
positively charged selected pairs,
                            ∫                    ∫
                               F (z, y(z)) dSz = F (z(y), y)Jy→z dSy ,

where the right-hand integral is over the selected positive feet, with null critical images omitted on
the left. This proves the claimed transfer for arbitrary nonnegative weights, not only constant ones,
and completes the proposition.

7.7   Defect and reach near bad zeros
We finish with the collar estimate used when geometric tests are joined to ambient tests. Its
source-reach bound also applies when the second contact, rather than the base, lies close to a bad
zero.
    For the next statement, a good zero relative to a set X ⊂ Σ means a point of Σreg \ X.

Corollary 7.8 (Reach bounds near bad zeros). For sufficiently large d(z), D(z) + a(z) ≤ CΓ /d(z).
If X = {z ∈ Σ : D(z) ≥ δ} with fixed δ > 0, then for every fixed L0 there is a finite uniform upper
bound for d(z) at all good zeros with dist(z, X) ≤ 10L0 . The same conclusion holds for a contact
reach d(z) when its second foot y satisfies dist(y, X) ≤ 10L0 .

Proof. Use the tangent balls of Lemma 7.1 and Lemma 7.6. The proof of Proposition 7.7 supplies
the corresponding tangent balls at second feet, with radius measured by the source reach d(z).
Exponential comparison in (5.9) bounds D below by a positive constant depending only on δ, L0
within distance 10L0 of X. Combining it with the appropriate upper bound CΓ /d(z) gives the
asserted reach bound. If X is empty the collar assertion has no points to consider.


8     Interface stability and exact interactions
We retain the notation and hypotheses of Section 7. Thus n = 7, m = 6, and the penalty Γ, including
its constant value G1 near 1, is fixed. None of the estimates in this section assumes an energy
density bound. The elementary local estimates below also hold in every fixed ambient dimension.
    Our objective is to turn ambient stability into estimates on the zero surface and to identify
the strength of the interaction between nearby zero sheets. Recall that D = 1 − |∇U |2 is a diffuse
scalar field, whereas a2 = |A|2 and H = tr A are, respectively, the squared norm and signed trace
of the second fundamental form of a regular zero sheet. These quantities have different roles: the
estimates below compare them, but do not identify them or assume that H vanishes.
    The argument has three parts. A finite normal average of q 3 D controls curvature and mean
curvature, and supplies an additional favorable term in surface stability. A comparison on both


                                                 49
sides of a sheet then relates D to curvature and the exponential of its penalized reach. Finally, on
logarithmic windows with uniformly small defect, we determine the sharp constants in the interaction
and phase-tail estimates. These are the surface inputs for the growth and radial arguments in
Sections 9 and 10. The passage from stable diffuse solutions to interacting transition layers is central
to the local theory of Wang and Wei [26]. Here we prove the surface test and exact interaction
estimates needed in the quartic normalization.

8.1     Normal averages, curvature, and mean curvature
Recall the normal average defined in (5.26): at a regular zero and for P > 0,
                             ∫                                              √
                            1 P                3                           4 2

Proof. It suffices to treat 0 < ε < 1, since decreasing ε strengthens the conclusions. The two-sided

    These limiting inequalities imply their stated finite-window versions. Here is the uniformity
argument for the curvature assertion, where an additive limiting error would be insufficient. If no
fixed interval and threshold worked, choose Pj → ∞, D(zj ) → 0, and IPj < (1 − ε)a(zj )2 . The
lower local mass bound gives a(zj )2 /D(zj ) ≥ c/(1 − ε) > 0. Subsequence convergence on every fixed
interval would contradict the preceding limiting inequality. Here a√2 /D converges to |𝒯 (0)|2 . Choose

the interval large enough also for Lemma 5.6 with coefficient 1/(2 2) + ε, and then take the smaller
of the two defect thresholds. Its nonnegative integrand preserves that mean-curvature bound on
every larger fixed interval. This proves all assertions with the stated order of choices.

    The next estimate concerns tangential variation of H. Its small coefficient is obtained by
averaging on a sufficiently large, but fixed, surface disk. It will allow the mean-curvature derivative
in the curvature equation to be absorbed in Section 9.

8.2   From ambient tests to surface stability
The preceding estimates concern one zero and a fixed normal interval. We now use them simulta-
neously along a surface test. Normal segments from different bases may overlap, so the ambient
test must account for all their inverse branches. Taking the square root of the sum of their squares
preserves the potential term exactly and produces a useful nonnegative difference of gradient energies.

applications below. At a = 0 take β = 1 and use the same capped limit.

form. The normal Jacobian supplies a2 /2, while the profile deficit supplies IP /2. Keeping both
terms will allow separate control of curvature and of interaction in the later estimates.

8.3   Comparison at the exact exponential rate
For 0 < δ ≤ 1/4 set
                                                {                       √

The zero set is closed, and X includes every singular zero. Here E records the interaction scale
associated with reach. To compare it with D, we must also retain curvature away from the base: a
local curvature value alone does not control the two-sided comparison problem. The exponentially
weighted supremum J0 in the next proposition records that curvature, with value 1 assigned to the
excluded set X.
Proposition 8.5 (Exact exponential comparison). There exist λ > 0 and C < ∞, depending on
the fixed penalty and dimension but independent of δ ∈ (0, 1/4], such that

                                                                               J0 (z) = sup e−λ|y−z| ā(y)2 .

                                                                                              √
    We prove the proposition with explicit comparisons. In particular, no loss in the exponent 2 is
absorbed into a constant. The lower bound for D comes from a positive kernel and the opposite
sheet. For the upper bound, we construct an isolated solution on each side of the same base graph.
Adding their equations cancels the terms linear in the graph’s mean curvature. The remaining task
is to compare each artificial solution with the true phase while preserving the exact exponential
rate across a growing chamber.
Lemma 8.6 (A half-space inverse). Let p0 > n, Vg = 3g 2 − 1, and Lg = −Δy − ∂t2 + Vg (t) on
ℝm × (0, ∞), with zero Dirichlet trace. For some b0 > 0, uniformly in 0 ≤ b ≤ b0 , its inverse maps
the norm

the functions to which we apply the inverse, rather than this comparison function, have zero trace.

energy estimates followed by interior and flat-boundary elliptic estimates and Sobolev bootstrapping
bound its W 2,p0 norm locally by the source’s Lp0 norm. These bounds are uniform in the block
location; only a fixed number of bootstraps is required because p0 > n is fixed.

Proof of Proposition 8.5. Fix z and write B = J0 (z) + E(z). We need only treat B → 0. For the
first inequality other values are trivial. For the second, if E ≤ J0 there is nothing to prove; if B is
bounded below and E > J0 , then d is uniformly bounded and D is uniformly bounded below. The
latter assertion follows from single-profile compactness: D(zj ) → 0 would make U converge to a
linear coordinate on every fixed ball, forcing d(zj ) → ∞ by the definition of the penalty. This is
also Lemma 7.1.
     Step 1: ordered sheets on the comparison scale. Translate and rotate so z = 0, ν(z) = en .
Put P0 = A0 log(1/B), where A0 will be large and fixed. After A0 is chosen, take λ so small that
10λA0 < 1/2. Every zero in B10P0 is then good, and

                                      |A|2 ≤ J0 (0)e10λP0 ≤ B 1/2 .

Indeed a bad zero there would give J0 (0) ≥ B 10λA0 > B. At every such good zero |∇v| ≥ 3/8, so
                                                                                               √

uniform elliptic estimates give uniform graph smoothness on a fixed scale.
     The base sheet extends over a disk of radius at least 2P0 in its tangent plane. To see this directly,
lift radial segments by tangent projection. Along a lift of length O(P0 ) the normal changes by at
most O(P0 B 1/4 ) = o(1), so the projection remains invertible and the lift length remains controlled.
All lifts stay inside the regular larger ball, and any terminal limit is regular. Continuation and local
uniqueness give a smooth graph; the same argument works for sheets based in a fixed small fraction
of BP0 . Their heights differ from their tangent planes by O(P02 B 1/4 ) = o(1).
     Here is the ordering argument with the length scales recorded. Fix a small angular parameter
θ > 0. A sheet based within θP0 has unoriented normal within Cθ + o(1) of en . Otherwise its
almost affine disk of radius comparable to P0 crosses the base disk transversally: along a line in the
direction of its tilt, its signed height relative to the base changes sign, with magnitude bounded
below by a constant times the tilt times P0 , while both graph errors are o(1). The intermediate
value theorem would give an intersection, impossible for two different regular zero sheets. Thus all
pieces in a smaller box are ordered graphs over one horizontal disk. There are finitely many in each
closed inner box: infinitely many would accumulate at a regular zero, where the fixed local graph
property permits only one piece. Descriptions on overlapping disks agree or are disjoint.

The terms ∓Δh g ′ cancel. Every remaining source is O(J0 (0)) in the weighted local Lp0 norm with
exponent 10λ: the coefficient factors have weight 2λ, the perturbations have weight 3λ, and the
cubic terms also have a uniform small bound on one factor. Taking 10λ < b0 and using the inverse
and boundary Sobolev embedding, we conclude

                                    |∂t (φ+ + φ− )(0, 0)| ≤ CJ0 (0).                          (8.11)

8.4   An integral comparison
The pointwise comparison contains the supremum J0 . For the growth argument we need an integral
estimate involving a4 , D2 and E 2 instead. Uniform local area and derivative bounds let us replace
the supremum by a positive convolution, with a small D2 error that can be absorbed. The constants
must remain independent of the eventual choice of the defect threshold and of the center of the
spatial weight.

Lemma 8.7 (Uniform area on good pieces). For 0 < δ ≤ 1/4, the area of Σ \ X in any unit ball is
bounded by a dimensional constant, independently of δ. Consequently its area in an ambient ball
of radius r ≥ 1 is at most C(1 + r)n . At a good zero whose distance from X exceeds 2, there √ is a
fixed-size graph neighborhood on which D is comparable to its value at that zero and |∇Σ A| ≤ C D.
                                 √
Proof. At a good zero, |∇v| ≥ 3/8. Uniform second derivative bounds for v give a fixed ball on
which its derivative along the initial normal is bounded below. All zeros in a smaller ball therefore
form one graph, with uniformly bounded slope and derivatives. A separated selection of such smaller
balls has bounded cardinality inside a unit ball, by ordinary ambient packing, and covers its good
zeros. Summing their graph areas gives the claim. Larger balls are covered by C(1 + r)n unit balls.
The last assertions are local Harnack and the differentiated bounds in Lemma 5.1, applied on a
fixed regular graph contained in the good set.

8.5   Sharp constants in logarithmic windows
The comparison above fixes the exponential rate, with unspecified multiplicative constants. On
windows whose defect decreases at a polynomial rate, the sheets are sufficiently close to parallel
planes to compute the coefficients as well. We first make the geometry and the additive distance
errors precise; these errors must tend to zero before they are inserted into an exponential.

Lemma 8.9 (Uniform geometry on clean windows). Fix ω > 0, C0 < ∞, and a fractional margin
0 < θ0 < 1. Suppose that z0 ∈ Σ and

                           D(w) ≤ N −ω            (w ∈ Σ ∩ BN ω (z0 )),      N −→ ∞.



                                                         61
The following conclusions hold uniformly for bases z ∈ Σ ∩ B(1−θ0 )N ω (z0 ). Local zero pieces within
C0 log N of z are finitely many ordered smooth graphs over Tz Σ. Their domains contain a disk of
radius cN ω/8 , with a further comparable graph buffer. They have curvature at most CN −ω/2 and
slopes and differences of unoriented normals at most

                                      C N −ω/8 log N + N −3ω/8 .
                                        (                           )


Distinct graphs have Euclidean separation at least c0 log N where they participate in these neighbor-
hoods.
    For every fixed k > 0, these descriptions extend over lateral disks of radius (log N )k and
through every fixed multiple of log N in normal depth. They contain every zero piece meeting the
corresponding smaller cylinder. Projection to each participating local graph is unique and smooth
there. Differences between its Euclidean distance and its vertical distance, and differences between
its height increments and those of its tangent plane, are additive errors bounded by

                            Ck N −c (1 + log N )Ck = o(1),         c = c(ω) > 0.

The same bounds hold uniformly as the center moves in the stated fractional interior. In particular
the lateral domains include (log N )2 boxes and disks of radius ℓ2/3 whenever ℓ = O(log N ). The
polynomial buffers and unique projections make these descriptions consistent on overlaps.
    If a distinct graph is within C0 log N of the base z, then

                                             d(z) = G1 ℓmin (z),

where ℓmin (z) is the shortest Euclidean distance from z to an adjacent graph. All thresholds may
depend on the fixed parameters and on k, but not on the solution or the base.
Proof. We establish graph continuation on a polynomial scale before using it on any logarithmic
scale. Put
                                κN = CN −ω/2 ,     r = N ω/8 .
At every zero under consideration, |A| ≤ κN and |∇v| has a fixed positive lower bound. The
fractional margin contains all balls of radius 100r used below once N is large. Starting at any such
zero, lift a straight radial segment in its tangent plane by tangent projection. As long as the lift
has length at most 10r, its normal changes by at most 10κN r = o(1). The differential of projection
therefore remains invertible, and the lifted length is at most twice the projected length. In particular
every segment of projected length at most r continues to its endpoint: a terminating sequence stays
in the larger ball, has a regular zero as its limit, and local inversion continues it. The lifting equation
depends smoothly on the segment endpoint. Its endpoints thus form one smooth graph over the full
tangent disk; local uniqueness identifies it with the original zero piece. Starting with a larger fixed
multiple of r supplies the stated buffer. Taylor’s formula on these graphs gives the affine error

                                            CκN r2 = O(N −ω/4 ).

    We next put nearby pieces over the same plane. Two such graph disks whose base points are at
distance b = o(r) have unoriented normal angle at most
                                                      b
                                                  (          )
                                              C         + κN r .                                    (8.17)
                                                      r
Indeed, if the angle were larger, a line in one tangent plane in the direction of maximal tilt would
make its signed distance from the other tangent plane take opposite signs, with magnitude greater

                                                        62
than both affine errors, inside a fixed smaller disk. Points of that graph project into the interior
of the other disk. The signed graph height, measured in the second disk’s normal direction, then
vanishes by the intermediate value theorem. At this intersection the two normals would still differ
by more than their variations CκN r, contradicting the unique tangent plane of the regular level
set. This argument also excludes differently tilted pieces of a single connected component. Taking
b = O(log N ) proves the claimed common-plane slope bound. Over a common smaller disk the
graphs either agree or are disjoint, and so are ordered.
    For completeness, no new sheet can enter unnoticed through the side of a logarithmic-power
box. A zero w with lateral coordinate |y| ≤ (log N )k and height at most C0 log N first has
b = O((1 + log N )max(k,1) ) in (8.17). This already gives a slope tending to zero polynomially and
permits its graph to continue to the normal axis through z. Its height changes on that continuation
by at most                 (                             )
                              (1 + log N )max(k,1)
                        C                          + κN r (1 + log N )k = o(1).
                                       r
Its intercept is therefore within (C0 + 1) log N of z for large N . Applying (8.17) again at this
intercept gives the refined slope bound already stated. Enlarging the fixed depth constant in a
buffered description therefore includes all pieces in the smaller box. There are only finitely many in
each closed inner cylinder: otherwise zeros on distinct graphs accumulate at a regular zero, whose
uniform local graph neighborhood contains only one zero piece. This is local finiteness, and imposes
no global bound on the number of sheets.
    The same estimates give the required additive accuracy. On a disk of radius L = (log N )k , the
error from a graph’s own tangent plane is at most CκN L2 . If ϑN = C(log N/r + κN r) denotes the
common-plane slope bound, changing from its tangent plane to the common horizontal plane costs
at most CϑN L + CκN L2 in height. Both quantities are bounded by a negative power of N times a
fixed logarithmic power. They tend to zero as numbers, rather than only relative to the gap.
    To check projections, write one graph as xn = f (y). For a fixed point x, half its squared distance
to (y, f (y)) has Hessian
                                    Id + ∇f ⊗ ∇f − (xn − f )D2 f.
On a buffered disk relevant to logarithmic depth this is positive definite, with eigenvalues 1 + o(1):
the slope tends to zero and |xn − f ||D2 f | = o(1). The same bound holds out to a fixed fraction of
the polynomial disk, since its height variation is at most CϑN r + CκN r2 = O(log N ). A minimizing
point cannot occur at that outer boundary, whose horizontal distance is polynomially large, whereas
the vertical competitor has distance O(log N ). Strict convexity gives a unique foot, and the implicit
function theorem gives its smooth dependence on x. The stationary equation bounds the horizontal
displacement of the foot by CϑN log N and the difference between true and vertical distances by
Cϑ2N log N , after harmless enlargement of the common slope bound. These are the asserted additive
errors. This projection is to a specified local graph, not to an entire connected component. Every
foot that can compete for the nearest zero lies within logarithmic distance, and hence belongs to
the buffered family by the preceding completeness argument.
    To prove separation, set r0 = c0 log N with c0 > 0 sufficiently small. Starting at any participating
zero, the exponential bound in (5.9) gives D ≤ CN −ω/2 throughout B4r0 . Consequently |T | ≤
CN −ω/4 there. Integration shows that p remains uniformly close to the initial unit normal, so its
component in that direction is positive. All zeros in a smaller cylinder then form a single graph. In
conjunction with the common graph description, this excludes a distinct sheet at distance less than
a smaller fixed multiple of r0 .
    Finally assume a distinct sheet lies within C0 log N of z. Its nearest foot is interior to the
polynomial graph disk, and the shortest chord is normal to that graph. The common normal

                                                  63
estimate puts its angle at z in the plateau of Γ, for large N . It supplies a penalized competitor of
size O(log N ). Every better competitor has chord length O(log N ) because Γ ≥ 1, so lies in the
same graph system. A chord of length l to the base graph has normal displacement at most CκN l2 .
Since Γ(s) ≥ 1/s, its penalized length is at least cκ−1
                                                     N ≫ log N , also in a diagonal limit. For every
other graph all chords cost at least G1 times their length, and its shortest chord attains this factor.
Only adjacent graphs can minimize, since a chord to a farther one crosses an intervening zero first.
This proves the exact identity for d. All graph identifications and projections on overlapping inner
windows agree by their local uniqueness; all construction scales fit within the original fractional
margin.

    The complementary estimate concerns the value of the phase between   √       the same sheets. Here

    For (8.19), change the sign of v if necessary so the phase between the sheets is positive. Separation

    In that proof the two principal coefficients Cj can now be chosen equal to 2 + η, with arbitrarily

with lateral faces, the true perpendicular projection is unique and remains in the polynomially
buffered clean window. The signed distance to its sheet is M + o(1): the affine error on the

Finally q = (1 − v)(1 + v) ≤ 2(1 − v); take η sufficiently small in terms of ε to prove (8.19).

   The two sharp coefficients concern different quantities: 32 bounds normal defect mass from
below, while 4 bounds the phase tail from above. The radial test will compare them on the same
adjacent gap. The fourth-order estimate proved next first supplies the control of rough regions
needed to make that comparison globally.


9    A fourth-order growth estimate
The reach and interface estimates are local. We now integrate them to bound the portions of the
zero set on which the radial argument will incur errors: large defect, appreciable curvature, or short
reach. Throughout this section v : ℝ7 → (−1, 1) is an entire stable solution of Δv = v 3 − v, D > 0,

                                                   65
and its energy density is unbounded, as in Sections 7 and 8. In particular m = 6. Recall that
a = |A|, d is the penalized reach, and G1 is the constant value of its angular penalty near normal
incidence. Write                                         √
                                                           2
                       K(R) = 1 + sup MR (x),        κ=      ,    E = e−κd .
                                    x∈ℝ7                 G 1

The target scale is K(R)Rm−4 for the fourth curvature moment and the squared defect and
interaction. Its gain of four powers over the weighted area scale K(R)Rm will pay for rough regions
and curvature errors in Section 10, even when K(R) grows arbitrarily slowly. There are three
steps. Simons’ identity reduces the curvature moment to a small multiple of the squared defect. A
single ambient stability test then supplies both surface mass and mass near bad zeros. Finally, a
multiplier for the reach inequality bounds the derivative cost of that test. The integral comparison
of Lemma 8.8 will close the three estimates.
    All surface integrals in this section use induced area. Fix a threshold δ ∈ (0, 1/4) provisionally
and define

Here IP is the normal average defined in Lemma 8.1; its finite depth P will be fixed below. Thus
S0 is the favorable mass in the strengthened surface inequality. The convention when X = ∅ is
NR = 0 and dist(x, X) = +∞. Since D = 1 at a singular zero, every such zero belongs to X. The
quantity NR records the bad set through any maximal unit-separated net, rather than requiring
that set to be smooth.

9.1   Simons’ identity and the curvature moment
We first estimate a4 with a coefficient of D2 that can be made arbitrarily small. The method combines
Simons’ identity, a strict Kato gain, and stability, following the curvature-estimate arguments of
Schoen et al. [23], Simons [24]. Our surface need not be minimal. We therefore retain both the
derivatives of H = tr A and the cubic-curvature error involving H; the averaged mean-derivative
estimate (8.2) will make their cost sufficiently small.
Lemma 9.2 (Curvature controlled by a small defect cost). For each ε2 > 0 there are choices of the
interface tolerances and δ > 0 such that, uniformly for all centers and sufficiently large R,
                          ∫             ∫
                    A4 :=      2 4

The constants remain valid on decreasing δ once the preceding interface tolerances have been fixed,
except for the displayed allowed dependence of the last two coefficients on δ.
Proof. The strict gradient gain lets Simons’ identity and surface stability control one another. We
derive that gain first, apply it away from X, and then charge the omitted collar to NR .

The Kato gain. We first record the algebra behind the strict gradient gain. If A is a symmetric
tensor and ∇A is totally symmetric, there are dimensional θ > 0, C such that
                                  |∇A|2 ≥ (1 + θ)|∇|A||2 − C|∇H|2 .                               (9.6)
For |A| = |∇A| = 1, equality in ordinary Kato requires ∇i A = bi A. Total symmetry, after
placing b in the first coordinate direction, forces A to have only its 11 component. Consequently
∇H = b tr A ̸= 0. Compactness of the normalized finite-dimensional tensor sets therefore gives a
strict Kato gap when |∇H| is sufficiently small; when it is larger, the last term in (9.6) pays for the
inequality. Homogeneity proves the claim. The weak gradient of |A| is zero almost everywhere on
its zero set, so this includes that set.

9.2   Gluing ambient and surface stability
The curvature estimate leaves a cost proportional to NR . To control it, stability must detect defect
near X, where a regular surface test is unavailable. The ambient amplitude q = 1 − v 2 does this.
Away from X, lifting the normal profile G(s) = 1 − g(s)2 supplies the stronger surface mass S0 . We
now combine those amplitudes into one physical test.

                                                  68
    Combining ambient and transition-set tests is also part of the free-boundary methods of Chan
et al. [5] and the smooth quartic three-dimensional argument of Liu et al. [15, Sections 3 and 5]. The
latter uses a sum of transition-profile derivatives and a linear joining to the ambient amplitude. A
related geometric stability inequality in Chan et al. [4, Proposition 4.7] uses a weighted ground-state
identity on the normal parametrizing manifold. Here we use a square root of a sum of squares and
retain its exact gradient variance. The following argument supplies the required estimates on our
six-dimensional interface.
    The cancellation is already visible for two nonnegative Lipschitz functions ξ0 , ξ1 on a Euclidean
open set. Wherever ξ02 + ξ12 > 0, differentiation gives
                                                                ⏐2
                                                                         |ξ0 ∇ξ1 − ξ1 ∇ξ0 |2
                                               ⏐ √
                         |∇ξ0 |2 + |∇ξ1 |2 − ⏐⏐∇ ξ02 + ξ12 ⏐⏐ =
                                               ⏐                ⏐
                                                                                             .
                                                                              ξ02 + ξ12

The identity holds almost everywhere with value zero on their common zero set. If ξ0 = h cos θ
and ξ1 = h sin θ for locally Lipschitz h ≥ 0 and 0 ≤ θ ≤ π/2, the square root is just h and the
variance is h2 |∇θ|2 . Thus it cancels the cost of changing amplitudes when the profiles agree. In the
construction below, q and the lifted G agree to a controlled error on the short normal segments
called accurate. The other short segments are charged to defect mass, while the long segments have
exponentially small tails. This is a change from an ambient description to a surface description
near bad zeros. The interpolation between neighboring sheet amplitudes in Section 10 is a separate
construction.

Admissibility and the full variance. The immersion need not be globally injective. To justify
the physical test without choosing global sheet labels, first cap |s| and physical support by smooth
cutoffs. The nonzero lifted support is then compact inside the immersion domain: |z| is bounded
because both |x| and |s| are bounded, and μ keeps the bases away from X. Fibers are discrete by
local invertibility and finite by compactness. Around any physical point finitely many local inverses
therefore contain all nonzero branches. A smaller neighborhood has no additional branch arriving
from elsewhere in the compact support. Thus F is locally Lipschitz, including where it vanishes,
and is a compactly supported stability test. Almost everywhere,

                                                     73
     The remaining rays. The cancellation has used at most one lifted branch at each physical point.
We now bound every other partition cost by defect mass or by the bad-net mass. For inaccurate
lifted preimages of depth at most M ,

                                         G(s)2 ≤ Cξ D(z)eξM .

If an ambient point has no accurate preimage, select a closest zero. When its distance is at most
M , that zero is regular and belongs to Ssw , since X is more than 2L0 away. Its ray is normal and
inaccurate, whether or not it belongs to the curvature cutoff. Indeed an accurate full normal ray
has β = 1 by the bound on |s|a(z) proved above. Its base in Ssw has μ = f = 1, so it would supply
an accurate lifted preimage, contrary to the case under consideration. The phase estimate (5.14),
with exponent loss at most ξ/2, gives

                                         q(x)2 ≤ Cξ D(z)e2ξM .

This ambient integral is bounded using the area formula for the full normal map on |s| ≤ M . One
may restrict it to any measurable closest-point selection; the resulting integral is no larger than
the integral over all eligible rays. The normal Jacobian is at most C(1 + M )m . Its critical images
have zero volume by the area formula, so no inverse chart or unique nearest point is required for
this estimate. Measurable selection exists since the zero set is closed and nearest points form a
nonempty compact set locally.
    If the closest distance exceeds M , use phase decay and the volume bound in (9.4). For these
long ambient tails use one fixed coarse exponent in (5.14), independently of ξ; only the short-ray
comparison above needs an exponent loss chosen in terms of ξ. For lifted rays of depth greater than
M , a contribution in 𝒲 requires sX (z) ≤ 3L0 + |s|; the same collar bound and the exponential tail
of G2 apply. Thus, for fixed dimensional N and c > 0, independent of sufficiently small ξ and of C1 ,
all costs in this comparison are bounded by

                             (1 + M )N e2ξM
                        Cξ                  Dsw + Cξ (1 + L0 + M )N e−cM NR .                   (9.15)
                                   L0
The Jacobian, the integration over depth, and derivatives of β only alter the fixed power N . Weight
shifts cost e2|s|/R and are absorbed in the tail exponent for all sufficiently large R.
    Fixing the logarithmic window. The order of choices is now decisive. The defect threshold and
its positive bad-mass coefficient cδ have already been fixed. Choose C1 > (N + 3)/c, then ξ > 0
with 2ξC1 < 1/4, then bξ > 0 as above, and finally let L0 be large. Both coefficients in (9.15) tend
                                                     −3/4
to zero; the first is bounded by a constant times L0 (1 + log L0 )N and the second by a constant
        −3
times L0 (1 + log L0 )N . They can therefore be absorbed by the already fixed positive multiples of
Dsw and NR . This proves (9.12) with a fixed threshold δ.

Conclusion of the gluing estimate. Claim 1 supplies the positive reserve, Claim 2 leaves only
the signed partition costs, and Claim 3 absorbs those costs with the variance. Stability of F and
division by σ therefore give (9.8). The remaining CL0 R−2 NR is absorbed for R large; the interface
tolerances and Young constants were chosen with room inside τ . This also proves the asserted
uniformity in the center and in f .

9.4   Integrating the reach inequality and paying for the feet
There are two kinds of cost to compare with the surface mass in (9.8): a source at the point where
the reach is differentiated, and a positive charge at a receiving foot of a plateau contact. We
estimate the local sources first, then integrate the inequality and transfer the foot charges using
their multiplicity-one bound.

Part IV
Automatic bounded density and stable rigidity
10     A radial threshold and automatic bounded density
We now exclude the unbounded-density alternative for a stable scalar solution v : ℝ7 → (−1, 1) with
D > 0. Throughout this section m = 6. The inputs are the reach inequality and its positive-foot
transfer from Proposition 7.7, the sharp interface estimates from Section 8, and the fourth-order
bound (9.1). All are uniform under translation. We will use this uniformity to choose the center of
a large ball after choosing its radius.
    The proof has two parts. First we compare reach and stability using the same surface amplitude.
The comparison forces a positive radial integral to be negligible. We then transfer the energy in
the selected ball to regular zero-surface area. Jointly injective normal tubes show that most of this
area has small reach, where the radial integral has a definite lower bound. These conclusions are
incompatible.

with MR ≥ K(R)/2, then translate that center to the origin. Define

10.2    Weight identities and fixed analytic choices
We now fix the analytic tolerances used in the comparison. First choose the small normal-interpolation
                                                                                                    −4
allowances quantified in Lemma√ 10.5. Choose the penalty Γ so that 1 ≤ G1 < 1.001 and η0 < 10
in (7.22)–(7.23), and set κ = 2/G1 . Prescribe the tolerances in (8.1), (8.18), and (8.19); then fix a

tolerances may be at most .001. The middle-phase estimate holds at its prescribed tolerance on
sufficiently large clean windows. Finally choose the defect threshold and the other constants in
(9.1). The interpolation half-width Asw = 3, depth constant M , and reach truncation constant M0
are fixed before the final increase of R. None of these choices uses a lower bound on the speed at
which K(R) diverges.

                                                    85
10.5     The local geometry for a global ambient test
The remaining task is to recover almost all of ℒ from ambient stability. We will extend the amplitude
ψ normally and interpolate between its values across a fixed-width middle band of each relevant gap.
This differs from the square-root gluing in Section 9: that construction proved the growth bound,
whereas we now use that bound to remove rough regions while interpolating the sheet amplitudes
themselves.
Lemma 10.4 (Geometry of the radial interpolation regions). Fix C0 < ∞. For all sufficiently large
R, the common graph and projection conclusions of Lemma 8.9 apply within distance C0 log ρ(z)
of every zero satisfying dist(z, Z) > ρ(z)β0 /3. They apply in particular at every base with mH ̸= 0,
uniformly for ρ ≥ R and independently of B. The polynomial graph buffers accommodate every fixed
logarithmic-power lateral disk. Besides the exact identity d(z) = G1 ℓ(z) whenever a distinct sheet is
at logarithmic distance, where ℓ(z) is the shortest adjacent Euclidean gap, the following conclusions
hold.
  1. Two sheet distances within a fixed bounded amount of the least distance can compete only
     across an adjacent gap, on opposite sides of the point. Three sheets cannot so compete.
  2. Fix a base zi and one normal side. Its only possible partner in a middle band is the first
     adjacent graph on that side. Write ℓij for the distance from the fixed base to that graph, and
     suppose ℓij ≤ C0 log ρ. Before the ray reaches that graph, at logarithmic depths in the stated
     window, its two sheet distances si , sj satisfy
                                                            d si − sj
                            si + sj = ℓij + oR (1),                   = 1 + oR (1).
                                                           dsi 2
       The ray traverses a fixed-width middle band at most once. The partner graph and ℓij are fixed
       although the projected receiving point can move on that graph.
All distance errors are additive and uniformly vanish. Buffered graph families and their projections
agree on overlaps.
Proof. Choose 0 < ω < min(α, β0 ), put N = ρ(z), and consider B2N ω (z). Since ρ is 1-Lipschitz, its
values there are N + O(N ω ). For large R this ball avoids Z, because 2N ω < N β0 /3. Every zero in
it therefore satisfies
                                    D < ρ−α ≤ CN −α ≤ N −ω .
Lemma 8.9 applies on a fixed fractional interior, decreasing ω if necessary to leave a margin. Every
fixed logarithmic-power disk and every fixed normal interval fit inside that interior. Its errors are
bounded by a positive inverse power of N times a fixed logarithmic power, so their supremum for
N ≥ R tends to zero. The sharp analytic estimates (8.18)–(8.19) apply with these same premises
after P and the depth constants are fixed.
    We verify the extra conclusions used to count bands. In a frame over one of the common tangent
planes, unsigned sheet distances differ additively by oR (1) from distances to the ordered graph
intercepts. Successive intercepts differ by at least c0 log ρ. Among ordered points on a line, two
whose distances are within a fixed amount of the least distance must bracket the point and be
adjacent; a third is farther by a diverging amount. The same assertions therefore hold for the true
sheets.
    Now fix a base and its positive normal ray, putting the base at height zero. The first adjacent
graph on that side has intercept l = ℓij + oR (1). Before reaching it, the graph estimates and smooth
distance gradients give
                                                       dsj
                             sj = l − si + oR (1),          = −1 + oR (1).
                                                       dsi

                                                      86
These are the asserted sum and derivative formulas. The derivative of (si − sj )/2 is positive, hence
a fixed-width band is traversed at most once. A farther graph cannot be the partner before the
first is reached, because the first intervenes and is closer. After crossing that first graph, it remains
nearer than the base graph on the relevant logarithmic ray. Thus the base cannot meet a second
band on this side. The negative side is identical. Finally, all descriptions concern the actual zero
pieces and their unique projections, so they agree on overlaps.

Labels in this formula are local and may be chosen in either order.
    All participating feet lie a logarithmic distance from x, so dist(zi , Z) > ρ(zi )β0 /3 for large R.
Thus Lemma 10.4 supplies the projection charts even when all projected amplitudes vanish. There is
no triple competition. Moreover 𝒰(−t) = 1 − 𝒰(t), so exchanging the two labels leaves the formula
unchanged. At t = −3 and t = 3 it equals the appropriate single-sheet value. Near a zero it uses
only the smooth projection, without differentiating that zero’s unsigned distance. The overlap
assertion of Lemma 10.4 therefore makes η̃ one locally Lipschitz function.
    Let ζ be a Lipschitz function equal to one for dist(x, Σ) ≤ M log ρ(x) and zero for dist(x, Σ) ≥
M log ρ(x) + 1, with bounded gradient. For example apply a fixed Lipschitz cutoff to dist(x, Σ) −
M log ρ(x). Set η = ζ η̃ in the displayed region and zero elsewhere. Near its auxiliary boundary
dist(x, Z) = ρ(x)β0 /2, every participating foot lies at distance less than ρ(zi )β0 from Z, hence has
mH = 0. There is a full zero neighborhood before this boundary, so extension by zero remains
locally Lipschitz.
    If η(x) ̸= 0, some participating base has |zi | ≤ 2B, and |x − zi | ≤ C(1 + log ρ(x)). Consequently

                                   |x| ≤ 2B + C(1 + log(R + |x|)),

                                                   87
which bounds |x| for fixed R, B. A finite cover of this compact support by the local projection
charts gives a finite Lipschitz constant. Uniformity of that constant as B grows is unnecessary. We
have constructed an admissible test for (5.8) without a global enumeration or a global bound on the
number of sheets.

10.7    The normal switching cost
Lemma 10.5 (Normal switching cost). With the preceding fixed parameter choices, for sufficiently

                                     ∫

Proof. We separate the terms whose coefficients tend to their planar values from the normal

Defect mass, tangential derivatives, and cutoffs. Over supported bases the fixed tubes
|s| ≤ P are jointly injective for large R: any intersecting segments have bases in one buffered chart,

One fixed base and one adjacent side. Fix an assigned base zi and one normal side that
contributes a band. Its partner is the first adjacent graph on that side, fixed throughout the band.
Let ℓij be the shortest Euclidean distance from this fixed base to that graph, and put ℓi = d(zi )/G1 .
By Lemma 10.4, ℓi is the shortest adjacent gap, so u := ℓij − ℓi ≥ 0. The projected foot zj moves
along its graph as the normal depth changes; ℓij does not.
    We justify the additive error for the moving foot. Put zi = 0 and its normal ray on the vertical
axis, so x = (0, si ) and si is the actual normal depth. Write the partner graph as x7 = f (y), with
common-plane slope bounded by θN = o(1) at a polynomial rate, where N = ρ(zi ). The stationary
equation for projection gives |yj | ≤ CθN log N and f (yj ) = f (0) + O(θN2 log N ). The logarithmic

lower separation then gives
                                                      2

after choosing the positive vertical side; the negative side is identical with absolute heights. The
second equality also follows by applying the same projection equation to the base zi itself. The
chord makes an unoriented angle tending to zero with both graph normals, so its penalty at zj is on
the plateau. It is an admissible receiving-reach competitor, giving
                                d(zj ) ≤ G1 (ℓij + oR (1)),       d(zi ) = G1 ℓi .                      (10.16)

    The vanishing error in this normalized assertion is uniform even if u grows with log ρ. For any

    The count of sides now matters. There is at most one first adjacent graph on each side, and
the normal ray crosses its band at most once. If both sides contribute, at least one realizes the
nearest adjacent distance ℓi ; if only one side contributes and the nearest side is different, that single
contributing side is the only possible positive charge. Thus at most one side needs the positive
supremum of (10.20). Restricting to the measurable part assigned to zi can only decrease the
integral. No factor of two multiplies this supremum. Figure 2 summarizes this count.

Every absorption of oR (1)ℒ above is performed at finite B, with its coefficient uniform in B. Thus

   It remains to find enough surface area where the weight has not decayed. The chosen center
supplies energy of order K(R)Rm . We first transfer that energy to regular zero-surface area, then
use injective normal tubes to exclude a substantial portion at large reach.

Lemma 10.6 (Area at small reach). For all sufficiently large R, the set

                                    {z ∈ Σ ∩ B2R : mH (z) = 1, d(z) ≤ L/2}

has area at least cK(R)Rm , with c > 0 independent of R and of the selected center.

Proof. The energy in BR is at least cK(R)Rm by the center choice. We first transfer a fixed fraction
of it to good surface area. Let 𝒵 be a fixed-scale separated net on all of Σ. Uniform gradient
bounds give a fixed positive lower energy in a fixed small ball at every zero, including singular zeros.
Packing, the definition of K, and ball coverings show that the number of net points in BR+j+2 is at
most
                                      CK(R)Rm (1 + j/R)m+1 .


                                                         92
By (5.14) and Modica’s inequality, the energy at points of BR whose distance from Σ is greater
than P0 is therefore at most

                                                (1 + j/R)m+1 (j + 2)n e−c1 j .
                                         ∑
                            CK(R)Rm
                                         j≥P0

Choose P0 large and fixed so this is a small fraction of the initial energy. Energy within the
enlarged rough collar is also a negligible fraction: its density is bounded and its volume in B2R is
O(K(R)Rm−4+2α+nβ0 ). Enlarge the collar by 2P0 without changing this estimate. Every remaining
point lies within P0 of a zero z in B2R with mH (z) = 1. Such bases have D → 0 uniformly. Their
fixed normal tubes are regular with bounded Jacobian, and cover the points in question by nearest
projection. Their energy is at most a fixed constant times their base area by the area formula and
the bounded energy density. Thus

                             ℋm (Σ ∩ B2R ∩ {mH = 1}) ≥ cK(R)Rm .

    The large-reach part can now be removed with the jointly injective tube estimate from Remark 7.2,
applied with threshold L/2. More explicitly, its normal map is injective simultaneously over all
bases with d > L/2 and on both normal sides for |t| < c2 L, where c2 > 0 depends only on the fixed
penalty. Its Jacobian has a fixed positive lower bound. Since L ≤ R, the images of bases in B2R lie
in a ball of radius at most 3R. Consequently
                                                                       √
          ℋm (Σ ∩ B2R ∩ {d > L/2}) ≤ CRm+1 /L = CK(R)Rm / K(R) = o(K(R)Rm ).

Subtracting this from the preceding good-area lower bound proves the lemma.
                                                                      √
   Take B > 3R in (10.24). On the set in Lemma 10.6, mc = 1, ρ ≤ 5R and Tr ≥ 1/5. Since
d ≤ L/2 and f∗ (cτ ) = 1,
                                         ∫ L

                             ℱ(τ, d) ≥          t−ϑ dt =              L .
