Also ϕTr /ρ2 ≥ cR−m on B2R . The left side of (10.24) is consequently at least cK(R)L1−ϑ = c𝒜.
First let B → ∞ at fixed R and then let R → ∞. This contradicts (10.24). The unbounded-density
alternative is impossible.

Completion of Theorem 1.2. If a solution attains either endpoint of [−1, 1], it is the corresponding
constant by the strong maximum principle. The remaining constant, zero, is unstable. Indeed, for
any nonzero χ ∈ Cc1 (ℝ7 ), the test χR (x) = χ(x/R) gives
                                              ∫           ∫
                                Q0 (χR ) = R5 |∇χ|2 − R7 χ2 < 0

for large R. For a nonconstant stable solution with values in (−1, 1), if D vanishes anywhere, the
equality case in Section 5 gives a planar heteroclinic. Otherwise D > 0 everywhere. If its centered
density is bounded, Theorem 1.3 applies. If that density were unbounded, then K(R) → ∞ and
Sections 7–9, followed by the contradiction just proved, would apply. Thus this last case cannot
occur, and Theorem 1.3 gives precisely the claimed constants and planar profiles.




                                                     93
A      The dimension-nine comparison
There are two dimension-sensitive steps in the proof. The classification of the end profiles used
Theorem 1.2 in dimension seven. The cone argument used the absence of a singular ray in ambient
dimension eight. In dimension nine, the singular-set bound allows dimension one, so a vertical
cylinder over a singular minimizing cone in ℝ8 is no longer excluded. The actual construction of del
Pino, Kowalczyk, and Wei realizes precisely this possibility.
     Here the numbering refers to their published paper [7]. Its Theorem 1, pp. 1488–1489, constructs,
for sufficiently small fixed α > 0, a bounded entire smooth solution uα in ℝ9 , strictly increasing in
x9 , associated with the nonaffine entire minimal graph
                         Γα = {(p, Fα (p)) : p ∈ ℝ8 },      Fα (p) = α−1 F (αp).
It satisfies
                                |uα (x)| −→ 1    as dist(x, Γα ) −→ ∞.                             (A.1)
The graph is a refinement of the Bombieri–De Giorgi–Giusti construction [3]. With p = (ξ, ζ) ∈
ℝ4 ×ℝ4 and (|ξ|, |ζ|) = (r cos θ, r sin θ), Lemma 2.1 and Theorem 2 of del Pino et al. [7, pp. 1492–1493]
give
                      F0 (r, θ) = r3 gBDG (θ),    gBDG (θ) > 0 (π/4 < θ < π/2),
               F0 ≤ F ≤ F0 + Cr−β min{F0 , 1}    in this sector for r > R0 ,       0 < β < 1,      (A.2)
                                          F (ζ, ξ) = −F (ξ, ζ).
The angular notation gBDG is unrelated to the heteroclinic g. In arXiv version 0806.3141v2 the
corresponding main and graph theorems are numbered 1.1 and 3.1, respectively.
    We spell out the consequences needed for the comparison. For a fixed base point p, continuity
bounds Fα on every bounded neighborhood of p. Hence dist((p, t), Γα ) → ∞ as |t| → ∞: otherwise
a graph point a bounded distance away would have a bounded base coordinate and an unbounded
height. Bounded strict monotonicity gives distinct vertical limits, while (A.1) puts both limits in
{−1, 1}. They are therefore −1 and 1. In particular −1 < uα < 1 everywhere and each vertical line
has a unique zero, of height hα (p). Equation (A.1) also implies that all these zeros lie within a fixed
distance Dα < ∞ of Γα .
    Fix p with 0 < |ξ| < |ζ| and fix t ∈ ℝ. The angular direction stays in a compact subinterval of
the positive sector for all points Rp + O(1) as R → ∞. The lower bound in (A.2), with α fixed,
gives
                        Fα (Rp + O(1)) ≥ cp,α R3 for all sufficiently large R.                     (A.3)
A graph point within Dα + 1 of the zero (Rp, hα (Rp)) has base coordinate Rp + O(1), so (A.3)
implies hα (Rp)/R → +∞. Thus uα (Rp, Rt) < 0 for large R. Moreover the distance from (Rp, Rt)
to the graph tends to infinity: a bounded-distance subsequence would contradict (A.3). By (A.1),
                                 uα (Rp, Rt) −→ −1       if 0 < |ξ| < |ζ|.
The oddness of F and the same argument give limit +1 in the opposite sector 0 < |ζ| < |ξ|. The
excluded equality set and coordinate axes have Lebesgue measure zero. Bounded convergence now
shows local L1 convergence of the rescaled solutions to the two-phase function with interface
                                     𝒞9 = {(ξ, ζ) : |ξ| = |ζ|} × ℝ.                                (A.4)
This cone has the entire vertical axis as its singular set and is invariant under vertical translations.
Its directional normal component is zero on its regular part. Thus it falls into exactly the case that
was ruled out by the singular-set bound in Lemma 3.4 in dimension eight.

                                                   94
    The same solutions are minimizers by Lemma 3.2, applied to their constant end profiles, and
consequently have the corresponding O(R8 ) energy growth. Thus neither the derived minimizing
property nor this energy bound conflicts with the construction. Their nonplanar phase limit also
precludes one-dimensionality. The construction extends constantly in additional coordinates to
every N > 9, as explained in del Pino et al. [7, p. 1490]: the added second derivatives vanish, and
strict monotonicity in x9 is preserved. It supplies no eight-dimensional example.
